#623. The blue wave has caused ripples of hetero-optimism

On Nov. 3, left-leaning Americans felt something that had lain dormant for a long time: hope.

The U.S. midterm elections resulted in historic wins, including Democrats flipping seats in several states. After the gut-punch of Trump's second win in 2024, and the subsequent human rights abuses and skyrocketing costs in the last two years, the "blue wave" (or bluenami, bluke, however you'd like to refer to it) swept over many of us like a calming balm.

The election results haven't just caused democratic optimism (or millennial optimism). For some women who date men, it has also bubbled over into romantic optimism.

AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections

Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

Pure — best for anonymous hookups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

"I'm hopeful that with a shift in politics and culture, the values of individuals and groups will start to shift too," Bailey Cummings, a 27-year-old communications and marketing specialist based in Nashville, told Mashable over Instagram DM.

The past few years have been less than stellar for heterosexuals. In 2019, writer and researcher Asa Seresin coined "heteropessimism," or feelings of shame, regret, and hopelessness when it comes to straight romance. The feeling seems to have only gotten worse since then, paving the way for 2025's "heterofatalism." The novelty of dating apps has long worn off, the debate over whether to engage in casual sex rages on, and having a boyfriend is embarrassing.

It doesn't help that young men and women have, at least in the past decade, held diverging ideological views. Men were becoming more conservative in the U.S. and elsewhere, and women more liberal. Politics are more polarized than ever, and the polarization carries over into our love lives.

"I'm on Hinge primarily, and if it says 'not political' or 'moderate' or 'conservative' in anyone's profile, regardless of their gender, I'm not gonna meet up with that person," said Ellie Rudy, a bisexual 27-year-old freelance journalist in Brooklyn.

Men under 30 had a 23-point shift to the right in the 2024 presidential election. But last Tuesday, they swung back nearly 40 points to the Democrats, the New York Times reported.

"After being told that men were basically a lost generation and that they'd been eaten up by the manosphere and Andrew Tate and were impossible to capture, we saw the opposite happen," journalist and author Liz Plank told Mashable.

Writer Magdalene J. Taylor proposed "hetero-optimism" in a New York Times op-ed back in May, but it's looking more relevant than ever.

That being said, Plank noted that while the results were uplifting, there's still a big divide between men and women.

A 2024 Financial Times article argues that the #MeToo movement, which peaked in 2017 and 2018, was the trigger for the gender divide among Gen Z. In the years since, differences of opinion have expanded beyond the issue of sexual violence and into politics (like immigration), marriage, and children.

Meanwhile, manosphere influencers like Andrew Tate sold a gender essentialist, traditional worldview to young men in which they are victims of feminism. Men, to Tate and others, should dominate women and be "alphas."

These misogynistic talking points align with the online incel (involuntary celibate) community, which rose from a fringe movement into the mainstream consciousness, especially since 2020. Incel forums fostered the looksmaxxing trend and its totem, the influencer Clavicular, who was recently charged with rape. As Mashable's deputy digital culture editor Olivia Tauber wrote in Sept., Clavicular built a following by humiliating women.

Far-right influencers aligned themselves with Donald Trump, and it's easy to see why women reject this outlook.

"What we've seen lately is this dominant political model of, 'This is what it means to be a man, and you have to behave this way, and we don't necessarily believe that women have the same rights or the same freedom to different roles,'" Sarah Ketay, a social and health psychologist at the University of Hartford, told Mashable. "My personal take is that a lot of women don't appreciate that."

And, regardless of age, the Pew Research Center found in a 2024 report that men are more likely than women to associate with the Republican Party.

But despite whatever far-right influencers spew, men and women aren't actually that different, Ketay argued.

"What we found in social psychology is that men and women often want exactly the same thing. They want partners who are kind to each other, who pay attention to them, who care about them," Ketay said.

The issues lie in how men are socialized, she continued. Generally, men are socialized to suppress their emotions except anger, and they're not taught to pay attention to how other people feel. This doesn't help heterosexual relationships. And these aren't inherent differences, Ketay said, but instead differences in how men and women are raised.

"Heterosexual relationships are actually at a disadvantage, not because of anything like biological, but because of how we're socialized," she said.

But there is good news: Positive qualities, like communication and awareness of other people's feelings, can be learned. And they don't have to be learned to be ready for a relationship, Ketay said; they can be learned while you're in one.

"I hope people can be a little bit more optimistic," she said, and some are.

Rudy told Mashable that she's cautiously optimistic. While she is disappointed in men she personally interacts with and men as a larger group, she feels hopeful. Though, she did point out that she's encountered leftist men who vote Democrat but act misogynistic and homophobic behind closed doors.

"There's something that's particularly frightening about liberal men who initially can make women feel safe, and then once you get to know them better, you realize it's all just a facade," she said. She's also dating more women than men right now.

Another bi woman, 31-year-old New Jersey resident Kaitlyn Porter, told Mashable over DM she doesn't feel more hopeful, because this is just the beginning. "We are leaps and bounds before men treat women as equals. I think it's nice to see them voting for the collective, but there's so much more that needs to be done," said Porter, a State Judiciary staff member.

Plank said there's a pessimistic view of the election that men thought gas prices were too high and decided to vote left, and it's not an ideological shift. But there's also a more optimistic view: That white men have realized that what Black women and other women have been organizing for decades to achieve can benefit them as well (as Plank wrote on her Substack).

While Plank believes men and women are in very different places, elections can give us a different understanding of a demographic we thought was unchangeable, or dynamics believed to be set in stone.

The 2026 midterms were a single election, and we don't know how it will impact us. But, given the policy platforms of the winners, there might be truth behind the "baby boom in 9 months" jokes.

"I think single straight women should feel more hopeful about all this!" Steffi Cao, a 28-year-old dating advice columnist and culture writer in New York City, told Mashable over Instagram DM.

Cao believes that the political divide between young, straight men and women is fueled by limited access to resources. These developments give her hope that change is possible. "Think about how the happiest countries are ones with strong social programs, and there's a correlation between marriage satisfaction and healthcare access," she said. Some Democrats, especially Michigan's Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, campaigned on Medicare for All this election cycle.

"I'm not saying that things will change overnight," she said, "but I am more hopeful that with these incoming structural changes, culture has more potential to shift as young men can realize it was never immigrants or women or trans athletes that were preventing them from their own success, but rather [the] system that promised them things and never delivered."

Ketay also mentioned that politicians and their social programs can help improve relationships and families. She cited New York City's free babysitting program, which provides childcare for a "parents' night out."

In recent years, singles have said that the rising cost of living is just another reason dating is harder. They can't afford dinner and drinks, let alone move out of their parents' home and buy their own.

But maybe politicians who want to get money out of politics, provide free healthcare, and tax billionaires could balance the economic scales somewhat and make every aspect of American life — including finding a partner — that much easier.

Anna Iovine is the associate editor of features at Mashable. Previously, as the sex and relationships reporter, she covered topics ranging from dating apps to pelvic pain. Before Mashable, Anna was a social editor at VICE and freelanced for publications such as Slate and the Columbia Journalism Review. Follow her on Instagram.

#621. A hidden limit of space and time could unlock electronics 100,000 times faster

Quantum physics places fundamental limits on what scientists can know about the behavior of particles. Werner Heisenberg's famous uncertainty principle states that certain properties, such as a particle's position and momentum, cannot both be measured with unlimited precision. This restriction is not caused by flaws in scientific instruments. It is built into the laws of nature.

But position and time are different. Heisenberg's uncertainty principle does not impose the same restriction on these quantities. Now, researchers have experimentally demonstrated another fundamental limitation: an electron's location and its behavior over time cannot both be determined with arbitrary precision. Known as the "space-time limit," this newly observed relationship could have major implications for the future of quantum technology and ultrafast electronics.

The discovery comes from an international collaboration involving research groups at the Regensburg Center for Ultrafast Nanoscopy (RUN), led by Profs. Jascha Repp, Rupert Huber, Franz Giessibl, and Klaus Richter, along with a team at the Max Planck Institute in Hamburg led by Angel Rubio.

Watching Electrons Move at Incredible Speeds

Understanding how matter behaves at the smallest scales is essential for developing the next generation of technology. Advances in clean energy, quantum computing, and powerful electronics for artificial intelligence all depend on knowing how chemical reactions unfold, how light interacts with materials, and how electrons travel through electronic devices.

However, even the sharpest microscopic photographs cannot reveal the full picture. Scientists also need to observe how these processes change over time, effectively creating slow-motion movies of events occurring at the atomic scale.

At RUN, researchers are developing ultrafast microscopes capable of recording the movements of electrons, atoms, and molecules with extraordinary precision in both space and time. About ten years ago, scientists in Regensburg used ultrafast scanning tunneling microscopy to resolve the movement of a single molecule across space and time for the first time.

Tracking individual electrons presents an even greater challenge. At these microscopic distances, electrons can move roughly a thousand times faster than atoms and molecules, with important changes taking place over just attoseconds.

To appreciate how extreme these scales are, consider that an atom is approximately ten million times smaller than a millimeter. An attosecond is one-billionth of a billionth of a second. The difference between an attosecond and a second is comparable to the difference between one second and the entire age of the universe.

Making these measurements is especially difficult because electrons do not behave like ordinary objects. Their motion follows the unusual laws of quantum mechanics rather than the familiar rules of classical physics.

Ultrafast Lasers Reveal Quantum Tunneling

To observe electron behavior at these extraordinary speeds, the researchers built a new laser system that provides much finer time resolution than earlier experiments. The system allows them to control and study the quantum motion of individual electrons directly.

In the experiments, carefully timed laser pulses drive electrons from an extremely sharp metal tip toward a silver surface. The distance they travel is only a few atomic diameters.

The researchers detect this movement by measuring the resulting electrical current. To determine exactly when the electrons move, they use two pulses of light separated by a precisely controlled interval.

Simon Maier, the lead author of the paper, explains: "By varying the time interval between the two laser pulses, we can directly observe how the electrons respond."

This approach revealed clear evidence of electron motion occurring on attosecond timescales. It showed that the laser pulses could transfer electrons within these incredibly brief intervals while allowing scientists to observe the process as it happened.

What makes the result particularly remarkable is how the electrons travel. Instead of moving like conventional particles, they behave as quantum waves. This allows them to pass through an energy barrier separating the metal tip from the silver surface, even though classical physics predicts they should not have enough energy to cross it.

This phenomenon, known as quantum tunneling, is somewhat like an object passing through a solid wall without breaking or damaging it.

"Our measurement can be understood as a high-speed camera for the electron wave packets, since you can see at what point in time the tunneling process takes place," explains Katharina Glöckl, a doctoral student and co-author of the publication.

To understand the measurements more deeply, researchers working with Prof. Angel Rubio performed sophisticated quantum simulations of the electrons' behavior near the "space-time limit."

Their calculations closely reproduced the experimental findings and uncovered another remarkable detail. The electrons did not respond to the light field instantaneously. Instead, their motion lagged behind it by approximately 500 attoseconds.

At such extreme scales, several fundamental features of quantum physics become important simultaneously.

For example, the researchers found that the laser pulses could not be described entirely by treating light as a wave or entirely by treating it as individual particles called photons. Their effects displayed characteristics of both descriptions. This combination was essential for exploring electron behavior so close to the "space-time limit."

The team also discovered that controlling electron motion over extremely short intervals changes how the electrons are distributed in space. Quantum mechanics describes these electrons using wave packets, which represent how their wave properties extend across different locations.

The relationship between the timing of electron motion and the spatial extent of these wave packets revealed the central limitation behind the discovery.

Raffael Spachtholz, another co-author of the study, explains: "The more precisely we want to pin down the electron's position in time, the more energy we need to provide. And as a result, the electron wave packet spreads out more spatially."

In other words, improving the precision of an electron's timing comes with a cost. The more tightly researchers define when its motion occurs, the more broadly its quantum wave packet spreads through space.

To examine this relationship experimentally, the team placed a single atom on the surface to confine the electron wave packets to an extremely small region immediately before the laser pulses arrived.

This setup allowed the scientists to directly measure the connection between the electrons' spatial distribution and the timing of their motion.

Importantly, even when the electrons received substantial energy from the laser pulses, their wave packets remained sufficiently localized for microscopy with atomic spatial resolution and attosecond time resolution.

The findings provide experimental insight into a fundamental space-time constraint that scientists had previously only suspected. They also make it possible to systematically investigate how changes in electron motion over time affect the spatial structure of quantum wave functions.

Could Quantum Electronics Become Hundreds of Thousands of Times Faster?

Beyond revealing a new aspect of quantum behavior, the discovery could open the door to technologies that operate on timescales far beyond the capabilities of today's electronics.

One especially promising possibility involves using precisely controlled electron wave packets to influence chemical reactions.

Transferring a single electron to a molecule represents the smallest possible transfer of electric charge. But when that electron is concentrated within an exceptionally small region of space and time, the resulting local peak current density can reach as much as 1 trillion amperes per square centimeter.

Such extreme conditions could give scientists new ways to control chemical bonds and observe how molecules change during reactions.

"In the future, we want to use such wave packets to specifically trigger chemical reactions and observe, on the relevant length and time scales, how chemical bonds can be broken or altered," explains Prof. Jascha Repp enthusiastically.

The implications could extend well beyond chemistry. Greater control over electron motion may eventually help engineers develop electronic devices and quantum information systems that operate at speeds approaching the fundamental limits of electron dynamics.

"In the long term, the insights gained could also contribute to operating electronics and quantum information processing at the intrinsic speed limit of electron motion itself -- hundreds of thousands of times faster than the currently dominant CMOS technology," adds Prof. Rupert Huber.

That possibility remains a long term technological goal rather than an achievement demonstrated in the current experiments. Nevertheless, the findings provide a new foundation for exploring how electrons can be manipulated at the smallest distances and shortest timescales.

For Repp and Huber, the results suggest that the range of potential applications is extraordinarily broad. They believe that future advances in controlling electrons at the space-time limit may ultimately depend more on human creativity than on the constraints imposed by nature.

#620. On this day in space! Nov. 10, 2013: Europe's GOCE satellite, the 'Ferrari of space,' fell out of orbit after mapping Earth's gravity field

Controllers weren't sure where GOCE would fall back to Earth. Some people worried that it might crash into a populated area.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 10, 2013, Europe's GOCE satellite fell to Earth after four years in orbit mapping the planet's gravitational field.

GOCE, its name was short for Gravity field and steady-state Ocean Circulation Explorer, launched in 2009 on a mission to map Earth's gravity like never before. But despite that successful mission, it was doomed in 2013 when it ran out of fuel.

At the time, controllers weren't sure where GOCE would fall back to Earth. Some people worried that it might crash into a populated area. However, the small spacecraft broke up in the atmosphere and didn't cause any property damage.

It disintegrated about 50 miles above the Falkland Islands in the South Atlantic Ocean. The European Space Agency called the mission a success. GOCE lasted far longer than expected. It showed small variations in Earth's gravity, mapped the structure of Earth's crust and mantle, and tracked ocean currents.

The GOCE satellite was a sleek spacecraft, with fins covered in solar panels that gave it a futuristic design. In fact, its look earned it a pretty cool nickname.

"GOCE's sleek, elegant aerodynamic design — responsible for it being dubbed the 'Ferrari of space' — immediately set it apart from most other satellites,." ESA officials said in a description.

There was more than looks behind GOCE's design. In order to generate the best map ever of Earth's gravitational field, GOCE had to fly in an orbit so low — just 139 miles (224 kilometers) — it needed to counteract the drag from the gossamer thin atmosphere of Earth at that altitude. The International Space Station orbits Earth at nearly twice that altitude.

GOCE was powered by an xenon ion engine that allowed to maintain its low orbit for four years. Once it ran out of fuel, the 1-ton satellite could not maintain its position in space, leading to a fiery plunge to Earth.

The large size of GOCE — it weighed 2,425 pounds (1,100 kilograms) and was 17 feet (5.3 meters) long and 3.2 feet (1 m) wide — made its reentry stand out, particularly since at least 25% of the satellite was expected to reach the Earth's surface.

#617. Scientists discover why some mosquitoes love you and others don't

Some people seem to attract mosquitoes wherever they go, but new research reveals that being a mosquito magnet depends on which species is doing the biting.

In a study published in iScience, researchers at Florida International University (FIU) tested how three of the world's most dangerous mosquito species responded to 119 human volunteers. Surprisingly, not a single participant attracted all three species. Instead, each species favored a different group of people, largely based on their individual body chemistry and natural skin odors.

The discovery could change how scientists approach the prevention of mosquito-borne diseases. Because different mosquito species respond to different chemical signals from human skin, researchers may eventually be able to create next-generation, species-specific repellents that offer more targeted protection.

"Isolating the components of human odor that attract or repel mosquitoes could lead to novel strategies to combat vector-borne diseases," said Matthew DeGennaro, a neurogeneticist who leads the research team and directs the Biomolecular Sciences Institute at FIU, Miami's state university.

Different Mosquito Species Prefer Different People

The researchers investigated three species responsible for spreading serious illnesses, including yellow fever, dengue, Zika, West Nile and other diseases. These were Aedes aegypti, Aedes albopictus (Asian tiger mosquito) and Culex quinquefasciatus (southern house mosquito).

Led by Ph.D. student Kaylee Marrero, the investigation was the first to directly compare how several mosquito species reacted to the same human participants. The findings revealed unexpected differences in the chemical and microbial signals each species appeared to use when choosing whom to bite.

"We didn't expect the species to prefer different people when we started the study," Marrero said. "Each species having a distinct microbial signature that they use as a cue was so surprising to me."

Each mosquito species showed its own distinct preferences:

The Hidden Chemistry Behind Mosquito Attraction

Human body odor is far more complicated than it might seem. Our skin releases a mixture of more than 1,000 volatile organic compounds, many of which scientists have yet to fully identify or understand.

The researchers found that the three mosquito species did not share a common preference for particular individuals. Instead, each consistently favored its own subset of volunteers, suggesting that different species have evolved distinct methods for detecting and interpreting human scent.

To investigate what made certain people more appealing, the team collected samples of participants' body odors and skin microbiomes alongside the mosquito preference tests. They then examined which chemical compounds and microorganisms were present and measured their relative abundance.

The results showed strong connections between the mosquitoes' preferences and differences in the chemical and microbial makeup of people's skin.

"This connected so well with the differences in skin bacteria and odors," DeGennaro said. "It is clear to me now that our skin microbiomes define our human odor signature. Each species found its own way to decode that signature."

Could This Discovery Lead to Better Mosquito Repellents?

Understanding why one mosquito species finds a person attractive while another does not could open new possibilities for preventing dangerous infections.

By identifying the specific chemical compounds and skin bacteria that attract or discourage different mosquitoes, the FIU researchers are helping establish a scientific foundation for more precisely targeted repellents.

The findings could also improve public health strategies by allowing communities to focus on the particular mosquito species responsible for spreading disease in their region.

Rather than assuming that all mosquitoes are drawn to the same people for the same reasons, future prevention methods may be designed around the distinct chemical preferences of each species.

#616. The 9 Best TV Shows to Stream This Month (November 2026)

With temperatures dropping, the holidays approaching, and relatives preparing to invade the sanctity of our homes, November is the perfect time to find a little peace and quiet in front of the TV.

Fortunately, this month’s lineup of must-watch shows offers plenty of excuses to escape, including a dramatization of one of the biggest scandals in cryptocurrency history, a return to the dystopian world of Blade Runner, and a dark comedy about desperate parents who turn to surveillance technology to find their missing daughter.

There’s also a return to Middle-earth, a deadly game of international espionage, and a mysterious archaeological discovery that sets off a globe-trotting conspiracy. Plus, we're revisiting two groundbreaking television series that helped shape our fascination with government secrets, surveillance, and conspiracy theories.

As always, we’re not just chasing the biggest names or the shows everyone is already talking about (as much as we do enjoy The Gilded Age). Instead, these picks reflect the kinds of stories WIRED covers every day, from technology and science to politics and culture, and the unexpected ways they often intersect.

Whether you’re in the mood for a mind-bending sci-fi thriller, a twisty international conspiracy, or a classic worth rediscovering, these are the TV shows worth making time for this month.

Ben Whishaw (Q in Daniel Craig’s Bond movies) is one of the finest actors working today, even if his name isn’t quite as recognizable as it should be. Fortunately, Black Doves gives him plenty of opportunities to remind us why.

The deliciously twisty British spy thriller stars Keira Knightley as Helen Webb, a seemingly ordinary wife and mother who just happens to be secretly passing government intelligence to a shadowy organization known as the Black Doves. Whishaw plays Sam, her longtime friend and a professional assassin who gets pulled back into her dangerous double life. Then there’s the always-brilliant Sarah Lancashire (Happy Valley) as Helen’s formidable handler, Mrs. Reed.

Season 2, which arrived November 5, raises the stakes considerably as Helen’s husband prepares to become Britain’s prime minister, putting her at the very heart of government. It’s a wickedly entertaining mix of political intrigue, espionage, dark humor, and double-crosses, with a stellar cast that makes even the most murderous characters hard to resist.

If you thought things were looking bleak in Middle-earth at the end of Season 2, just wait. Amazon's spectacularly ambitious—and wildly expensive—prequel to J.R.R. Tolkien’s The Lord of the Rings returns for a third season, picking up five years after the fall of Eregion. Sauron (Charlie Vickers) has been busy building his armies and conquering much of Middle-earth, leaving the Elves, Dwarves, Men, and Wizards scrambling to stop him. Meanwhile, the Dark Lord is working on his most dangerous creation yet: the One Ring.

The new season promises plenty of political intrigue, epic battles, and the kind of jaw-dropping fantasy world-building that makes the series such a visual feast. Even if you know where Tolkien’s story ultimately leads, watching the pieces fall into place is half the fun. The first four episodes arrive November 11, with the remaining four rolling out over the following two weeks.

Remember when cryptocurrency was going to change the world? For a while, Sam Bankman-Fried seemed poised to make that happen. The young entrepreneur became a billionaire seemingly overnight as the founder of FTX, a cryptocurrency exchange that promised to revolutionize finance. Then it all came crashing down in spectacular fashion, exposing an $8 billion fraud that landed Bankman-Fried in prison for 25 years.

The Altruists dramatizes that astonishing rise and fall, with Anthony Boyle (Say Nothing) playing Bankman-Fried and Julia Garner (Ozark) portraying Caroline Ellison (no relation to Larry Ellison), his on-again, off-again girlfriend and the CEO of his trading firm, Alameda Research. Inspired by reporting from New York magazine, the eight-part limited series explores how two young idealists who embraced the philosophy of effective altruism—using wealth and resources to do the most good—became central figures in one of the biggest financial scandals in recent history. All eight episodes premiere November 19.

If Amy Poehler and Mike Schur taught us anything with Parks and Recreation, it’s that even the most mundane workplace can be a breeding ground for brilliant comedy. Their latest collaboration takes that idea to an archaeological dig in Greece.

Based on Kate Myers’ 2023 novel Excavations, the 10-episode comedy stars Poehler as Elise, an ambitious archaeologist who has spent years waiting for her boss (Hugh Laurie) to retire so she can take over the operation. When she and three fellow excavators (Geraldine Viswanathan, Antonia Thomas, and Fina Strazza) uncover a mysterious artifact that could rewrite history, they make the questionable decision to keep it secret. Naturally, things spiral out of control, drawing the women into an international conspiracy that threatens their careers—and possibly much more. With a cast that also includes What We Do in the Shadows’s Kayvan Novak (aka Nandor the Relentless), Dig delivers an entertaining mix of workplace comedy, historical mystery, and archaeological intrigue. All 10 episodes premiere November 23.

More than 40 years after Ridley Scott’s Blade Runner first introduced audiences to a dystopian Los Angeles, where synthetic humans are almost indistinguishable from the real thing, the franchise is still daring to ask science fiction’s most unsettling question. In this case: What makes us human?

Set 50 years after the events of Denis Villeneuve’s excellent Blade Runner 2049, this eight-part limited series stars Hunter Schafer (Euphoria) as Cora, a fugitive who assumes the identity of a Blade Runner, a special operative tasked with hunting down rogue Replicants. She’s forced to team up with Olwen (Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant whose days are numbered, to track down another fugitive whose secret could unravel the very foundations of their society. With artificial intelligence advancing at a dizzying pace in the real world, the franchise’s questions about consciousness, identity, and the increasingly blurry line between humans and machines feel more relevant than ever. All eight episodes arrive November 25.

How far would you go to find your missing child? For Dedo (Axel Stein) and Carlotta Klatt (Heike Makatsch), the answer is: very far. And well beyond the boundaries of what’s legal. When their teenage daughter, Wanda (Lea Drinda), disappears without a trace, the desperate German couple takes matters into their own hands, enlisting their tech-savvy son to help them secretly install surveillance equipment throughout their seemingly sleepy suburban neighborhood. What they discover is that practically everyone has something to hide.

The truth is still out there. More than three decades after The X-Files premiered, Chris Carter's groundbreaking sci-fi series remains one of television’s most addictive explorations of government conspiracies, alien encounters, and things that go bump in the night (or creepily squeeze through air vents). It also made pop culture icons of David Duchovny and Gillian Anderson as FBI agents Fox Mulder, a true believer in the paranormal, and Dana Scully, a scientist and skeptic whose job was ostensibly to debunk his theories. Anderson’s character also helped to inspire a whole generation of women in STEM, a phenomenon known as “The Scully Effect.”

The pair’s chemistry, and seemingly endless will-they-or-won’t-they relationship, helped sustain the show through 11 seasons (including a two-season revival beginning in 2016), two movies, and countless monsters of the week. With Ryan Coogler’s (Sinners, Black Panther) reboot, starring Danielle Deadwyler and Himesh Patel, recently receiving a series order at Hulu, now seems like the perfect time to revisit the original.

Twenty-five years ago this November, Kiefer Sutherland introduced the world to Jack Bauer, a counterterrorism agent who could save the country from almost certain destruction in a single day—but never seemed able to find five minutes to use the bathroom. Premiering just two months after 9/11, 24 became a defining television series of the post-9/11 era, with its real-time format, ticking digital clock, and seemingly endless cliff-hangers making it nearly impossible to watch just one episode.

Each season follows Bauer through a particularly hellish day as he races to thwart terrorist plots, uncover government conspiracies, and navigate an increasingly murky moral landscape. The Emmy-winning thriller also gave us one of television’s most recognizable phone ringtones (courtesy of the fictional Counter Terrorist Unit) and some truly bonkers subplots—including a memorable Season 2 encounter between Bauer’s daughter, Kim (Elisha Cuthbert), and a cougar. Yes, a cougar. Some of the show’s politics and attitudes toward torture haven’t aged particularly well, but its influence on modern action television is undeniable. All nine seasons are available to stream.

This acclaimed British prison drama anthology explores the lives of inmates and correctional officers, with each season tackling a different part of the criminal justice system. The first season starred Game of Thrones’ Sean Bean as a guilt-ridden prisoner and Adolescence’s Stephen Graham as a morally conflicted prison officer, while the second shifted to a women’s prison, with Thirteenth Doctor Jodie Whittaker and The Last of Us’s Bella Ramsey leading the cast.

The third installment stars Doctor Who icon David Tennant as a veteran officer at a juvenile detention facility, where a violent incident raises questions about accountability, rehabilitation, and the psychological toll of incarcerating teenagers. Siobhan Finneran (Downton Abbey) returns as a prison chaplain, the only character to appear in all three seasons.

#612. DNA on the Shroud of Turin reveals centuries of hidden secrets

Scientists have uncovered a remarkable collection of DNA hidden within the Shroud of Turin, revealing a surprisingly complex biological history behind one of the world's most mysterious and controversial religious artifacts. The genetic evidence includes traces of humans, bacteria, fungi, plants, animals, and even Mediterranean red coral, offering new clues about the environments and people that have come into contact with the famous cloth over the centuries.

Published in Scientific Reports, the study used advanced DNA sequencing technology to examine samples collected from the Shroud in 1978. For the first time, researchers applied a sophisticated technique (rigorous PCR-free next-generation metagenomic sequencing) to these officially collected samples, allowing them to identify genetic material preserved within the linen fibers in unprecedented detail.

Unlike traditional DNA testing, which often focuses on genetic material from a single organism, metagenomic sequencing can detect DNA from many different species within the same sample. This makes it particularly useful for studying historical objects that have been handled, stored, and exposed to changing environments over long periods.

By examining this genetic material, the international research team assembled what they describe as the most comprehensive picture yet of the biological traces accumulated on the Shroud throughout its history.

Human DNA and a Hidden World of Microorganisms

Among the discoveries were multiple lineages of human mitochondrial DNA, suggesting that genetic material from different human populations has accumulated on the cloth. Mitochondrial DNA is a small portion of our genetic material that is inherited through the maternal line, making it especially useful for studying ancestry and population history.

The researchers also identified an unexpectedly diverse microbiome, the collection of microscopic organisms associated with a particular environment or object. These included bacteria commonly found on human skin, various fungi, and microorganisms adapted to living in salty environments.

Some of these organisms belong to a group known as archaea, which are single-celled microorganisms distinct from bacteria. Certain archaea can thrive in extremely salty conditions that would be difficult for many other forms of life to tolerate.

Together, these findings reveal that the Shroud carries a complicated biological record shaped by repeated human contact and exposure to different environmental conditions.

DNA From Plants, Animals, and Mediterranean Coral

The discoveries extended far beyond human DNA and microorganisms. Scientists also detected genetic traces from numerous plant and animal species, adding another layer to the Shroud's biological history.

Plant DNA included cultivated wheat, carrots, maize, bananas, and peanuts. Genetic material from domesticated animals was also present, including cattle, pigs, chickens, dogs, and cats.

One particularly unusual finding was DNA associated with Mediterranean red coral.

The wide variety of biological material suggests that the cloth has encountered a diverse range of living organisms and environmental influences. These genetic traces may reflect its long history of handling, storage, and movement through different geographical regions.

Importantly, the presence of DNA from a particular species does not establish when that material reached the cloth. Some traces could have accumulated relatively recently, while others may reflect much older interactions. This makes the findings valuable for reconstructing the Shroud's environmental history, but also highlights the difficulty of interpreting DNA recovered from an object that has been exposed to human activity for centuries.

Can DNA Solve the Mystery of the Shroud of Turin?

Despite the remarkable diversity of genetic material, the researchers emphasize that their findings cannot establish the Shroud's age or authenticity.

The Shroud of Turin is a linen cloth bearing the faint image of a human figure and has long been associated by some believers with the burial of Jesus. Its origins have been debated for generations, making it one of the most extensively investigated historical and religious artifacts in the world.

Because the cloth has been touched and exposed to different environments over many centuries, its DNA represents a mixture of biological material accumulated at different times. This makes it impossible to reliably separate any original DNA that might have been associated with the fabric from the many genetic traces deposited afterward.

Instead, the research demonstrates how modern forensic genomics and proteomics can reveal information that traditional methods might miss. Forensic genomics uses genetic evidence to investigate biological origins and interactions, while proteomics examines proteins, which can also preserve valuable clues about biological materials.

Together, these approaches offer powerful new ways to investigate how historical objects have been preserved, handled, and exposed to different environments.

Modern DNA Technology Reveals New Historical Clues

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, contributed to the international research collaboration. She emphasized that advances in forensic technology are making it possible to recover information from historical samples collected decades earlier.

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, said: "The Shroud of Turin continues to fascinate historians, scientists and the public, and we're thrilled to reveal new information about the biological complexity preserved within samples collected almost 50 years ago by applying new cutting-edge forensic DNA technologies.

"The Shroud represents a rich archive of genetic information that has accumulated over centuries of human interaction and environmental exposure. While the DNA evidence cannot answer all the questions about the age or authenticity of the cloth, it provides novel insights into its biological history and demonstrates how advances in forensic science can unlock new information from historical artifacts."

Materials provided by University of Lancashire. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#611. A transitioning Congress finds itself in a pickle on AI data centers

As Congress returns to Washington this week, the massive power demands of the AI boom have forced a reckoning over data center infrastructure.

The Senate is preparing for a pivotal procedural vote on legislation that would speed approvals for energy projects while requiring large data centers to shoulder more of the costs they impose on the power grid. The bipartisan bill would add much-needed regulations on data centers, but, as some politicians believe, may also encourage more data center construction.

In the sweeping and history-making midterm elections last week, voters didn't just elect a new generation of Democratic lawmakers. They also sent a pretty unmistakable message to Big Tech, particularly the artificial intelligence industry: Enough with the data centers already. Or, more bluntly, "get out of town."

Across the country, local communities are pushing to stop, restrict, or impose conditions on data center development. The election results reinforced that message, making clear that the AI gold rush is facing growing resistance.

For the last two years, Silicon Valley has busied itself building more, building bigger and doing it ASAP. If a tech giant wanted to drop a warehouse-sized data center into a community, local governments often offered tax breaks and other incentives, then told residents it was harmless and would mean more jobs.

Democrats now find themselves in a pickle. Many campaigned on restricting data center development, responding to voters concerned about electricity costs, water consumption, and the impact these enormous facilities have on their communities.

Now, the aforementioned pending legislation forces a stark about-face — since, as Reuters reports, some Democrats believe provisions in the proposed bill could encourage the creation of more data centers. If that comes to pass, incoming lawmakers would need to explain why those campaign promises to halt data center construction no longer apply.

There's a risk, too, in the new Democratic Congress pushing too forcefully for restrictions on AI, specifically on data centers. My well-seasoned brain believes AI companies will respond to restrictions not simply by complaining about the cost of doing business, but by slowing down the rollout of new technology.

Some of that slowdown will be unavoidable. Without enough data centers, electricity, and computing capacity, companies cannot expand indefinitely. But some of it could be strategic.

I suspect the message from Silicon Valley will be slightly PR-ish: If you don't want us building data centers, don't expect your ChatGPT, Claude, or Siri to keep getting faster, smarter, and more capable. That "hurry up and now" diagnosis for your sore knee might take a little longer.

Picture AI as something other than a magical cloud floating above our heads. Every time you ask an AI assistant to write an email, generate an image, or summarize a document, powerful chips in actual buildings are cranking away, consuming tremendous amounts of electricity and water and pressuring local power grids.

And all that power costs mega cash. Utility rates, grid upgrades, and higher electricity bills have turned an abstract technology debate into something much more personal. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

I don't blame communities for pushing back. Why should residents subsidize a multibillion-dollar technology company's expansion while wondering whether their electric bills will keep climbing? They're already fed up with rising gas and grocery prices and the cost of a new home.

But there's another side to this costly coin that Democrats shouldn't ignore. Data centers can bring economic benefits, too, provided technology companies invest in the communities that host them.

While data centers rarely employ local, low-skill workers directly, AI companies can bridge this gap by funding local infrastructure projects that immediately put the existing workforce to work. Furthermore, the resulting influx of permanent, high-salaried technicians injects new tax revenue into the community, triggering a powerful economic ripple effect for local restaurants and shops.

Communities shouldn't have to choose between protecting residents and benefiting from technological investment. Companies that want to build in these towns should contribute meaningfully to their economic well-being.

Government intervention, even when well-intentioned, can also produce unintended consequences that ultimately hurt consumers.

Just look at Europe. In 2024, Apple said it would hold back Apple Intelligence and other new features from EU customers, citing "regulatory uncertainties" tied to the Digital Markets Act. Regulators were trying to rein in Big Tech and protect competition. Whether you buy Apple's explanation or see it as a pressure tactic, European customers were left waiting while others got the shiny new toys.

This doesn't mean all regulation is bad. It's a reminder that lawmakers cannot promise to put a tight leash on an industry without potentially creating bottlenecks in the products and services it provides. That's precisely the dilemma Democrats now face.

If they make it too difficult or expensive to build data centers, AI companies will have fewer places to house the computing power necessary to expand their products. New features could take longer to arrive. More sophisticated applications could cost more.

And I wouldn't be surprised if the industry makes sure consumers understand exactly who's responsible. They'll be smug about it: "OK, you wanted restrictions. You got them. Now don't lose it when your AI assistant takes longer to answer. Or, we might have you paying a monthly fee."

It's a convenient argument for Big Tech, shifting attention away from an industry that hasn't always sought communities' permission or given much thought to ordinary consumers.

That's why Democrats need to be thoughtful about what comes next. Protecting communities, keeping electricity affordable, and demanding that tech companies pay their fair share are entirely reasonable goals. But a political victory over data centers could become a consumer defeat if lawmakers don't consider the consequences.

This article represents the opinions of its writer.

John Casey is a columnist at Raw Story, the Advocate, Daily Beast, and Outsports. He's also the author of Love: The Heroic Stories of Marriage Equality.

In any respectable audiobook project, finding the names of Andy Weir ("The Martian," "Project Hail Mary") and Benjamin Percy ("The Ninth Metal," "Wolverine") attached would elicit intense fan interest, but add the superstar vocal talents of Anthony Mackie ("Avengers: Infinity War," "Avengers: Doomsday"), Ariana DeBose ("West Side Story"), and John Leguizamo ("Ice Age"), and it catapults this sonic odyssey into the stratosphere.

Such is the case with "Exoplanet," Audible Original’s new 7-episode science fiction audio drama executive produced by Weir and penned by Percy. Its planet-hopping space disaster narrative sees the crew of the starship Wild Blue Yonder hit a quantum anomaly, forcing their ship to crash-land onto a deadly exoplanet. All is not as it seems, though, as each crew member arrives in a different era on the same world.

We hooked up with Mackie, DeBose, and Lequizamo to learn more about this renaissance of audio dramas and what attracted them to board Weir's sci-fi thriller.

One crew. One planet. Three timelines. No one survives alone. A sci-fi thriller executive produced by Andy Weir (#1 bestselling author of Project Hail Mary, The Martian), with an all-star cast led by Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose and John Leguizamo.

Exoplanet is free for Audible members, but also available for purchase separately.

Mackie voices Chief Engineer Jeff Shaw. His attraction to the imaginative genre began at an early age thanks to some cool family friends while growing up in the Big Easy.

"My brother went to college at Morehouse," he tells SPACE. "I was like 8, and he was 18. He’d always bring friends home, because New Orleans is a party city, so they'd come over and get drunk. But one of his friends gave me a book by Walter Mosley and one by Octavia Butler, and I got hooked into sci-fi and the future."

"From 'Back to the Future' to 'Aliens,' the future was such an idea of what was going to be out there. Now we’re living it," reveals Mackie. "It’s very important to me as a human being to have that construct as a part of my career. The only thing more arrogant than wanting to be president is standing up and saying, 'Yeah, I can go to space.' That sounds crazy to me."

Leguizamo's New York City childhood was influenced by absorbing classic radio dramas of the past, which primed him for this special Audible project.

“It was Anthony and Ariana being in it that really pumped me up, and of course Andy Weir," he recalls regarding his portrayal of Science Officer Logan Bowers. "Then when I got the theme, that it was something relevant today and makes you think about computers and technology taking over and making us obsolete. It just hit me in that right zeitgeist, at that right little point."

"As a poor kid, there was no YouTube or cell phones, and we didn’t have computers," reminisces Leguizamo. "I used to go to the Broadcast Museum on 53rd Street and listen to old radio plays like The Shadow and these incredible detective radio plays. It's a different art form. With no visuals, your voice has to create the world. I thought that was an incredible challenge."

DeBose portrays the Wild Blue Yonder's confident First Officer Becca Bang, who’s stranded in a temporal mystery that might engulf her at any moment.

"I've yet to do anything like this, which is always exciting," she adds. "I was a little afraid of it, which told me I should do it. These gents are so talented. It's great company. It’s coming from brilliant minds who build worlds so impeccably. And it's about real stuff we’re dealing with today. I liked the idea that we could tell this story and make people listen and create a space for them to imagine themselves in it and see how they problem-solve to make the world a bit better."

Recording your character's lines is far more difficult than just stepping up to the microphone to read a script, and our ace "Exoplanet" trio delivered in every session.

"I just go in and throw myself at something," DeBose recalls. "I don’t really have a method. But I can tell you this: it is a full-body workout. I use everything at my disposal, whether it’s a chair or a pillow or running in place. I just do what the character does and end up sweating. And after three days, I have to go sit in silence. It's great. I love the idea of bringing back the radio play. The art of listening is such a thing. I don’t think people listen enough. Even for myself."

Likewise, Leguizamo embraced his booth sessions with exuberance and authenticity.

"You have to cancel the rest of your day, especially if you've got to talk, because your vocal chords are fried," he admits. "And if they're not fried you're not doing your job. You really have to do the physicality. If you run you have to run like a maniac and be out of breath. If I'm getting hit I start punching myself. Then there’s a sexiness to doing these things. Your voice has to have a little mystery, a little naughtiness. It’s an interesting intersection where you pull it back a little and invite the audience in.”

For Mackie, who’s used to athleticism needed to play MCU superheroes like The Falcon and Captain America, recording dialogue offered a different type of opportunity.

“I look for reasons not to talk to people, so I love doing these," he jokes. "Once I’m out of the studio, I’m not talking to anybody for two days. At a certain point for me, I find mentally I get tired of hearing myself talk. I just have to come home at cut off. I’ve said enough words for the next month.”

"Exoplanet" is available now exclusively from Audible.

#608. Astronomers discover hidden alien planet in James Webb images

The Milky Way is believed to contain billions of planets beyond our solar system. These distant worlds, known as exoplanets, offer astronomers an opportunity to explore other planetary systems, identify potentially habitable environments, and perhaps eventually discover evidence of life elsewhere in the universe.

Finding these planets is one challenge. Actually seeing them is another. Most exoplanets orbit stars that shine so brightly that the planets become nearly impossible to distinguish from the surrounding glare. Even the most advanced space telescopes struggle to separate the faint light of a distant planet from the overwhelming brightness of its host star.

Now, researchers have developed a powerful new method for removing that starlight from telescope images, revealing planets that were previously hidden. The technique uses a sophisticated computer simulation of the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) and has already exposed an exoplanet that was concealed in existing observations. In tests involving known planets, it achieved two to 15 times greater detection sensitivity than conventional approaches.

A Digital Twin of James Webb Reveals Hidden Planets

The breakthrough centers on a virtual replica of Webb's Near Infrared Camera (NIRCam), an instrument designed to observe the universe in infrared light. By recreating how the camera operates, researchers can identify and correct tiny imperfections that interfere with its observations.

The virtual model, often called a "digital twin," allows scientists to simulate the telescope's behavior and determine how starlight spreads through its optical system. Once they understand the patterns produced by the star, they can digitally subtract much of that glare, making faint planets easier to detect.

"Directly imaging exoplanets is incredibly challenging because even the tiniest imperfections in a telescope can make it difficult to separate the faint light of a planet from the overwhelming glare of its star," explains Caltech's Katie Bouman, professor of computing and mathematical sciences, electrical engineering and astronomy, a Rosenberg Scholar, and a Heritage Medical Research Institute Investigator, who is a co-author of a Nature paper about the new method published online November 4.

"What's exciting is that we're showing how computation can help overcome limitations of the telescope itself," Bouman continues. "By combining a physical model of the instrument with optimization techniques from machine learning, we have been able to remove starlight much more precisely, making it easier to detect faint exoplanets and even reveal one that was previously hidden in the JWST data."

How Scientists Make Blinding Starlight Disappear

The researchers named their approach Physics-based Learning for Imaging Planets (FLIP). Rather than relying entirely on improvements to telescope hardware, the system uses advanced computation to correct problems in images that have already been collected.

To develop FLIP, the team created a detailed physical model of Webb's infrared camera. They then used computational techniques to examine millions of possible imperfections, subtle changes, and other unknown factors that could influence the telescope's observations.

These small variations matter because even an extremely slight distortion can alter how a star appears in an image. When astronomers attempt to remove the star's light, any remaining glare can overwhelm the much dimmer signal from a nearby planet.

By accounting for these effects, FLIP produces a highly precise model of the star's appearance. Researchers can then subtract that modeled starlight from the original observations, allowing previously obscured objects to become visible.

"This is the first time that we have demonstrated that these little imperfections or instabilities are not a limiting factor in the kinds of planets we can image," says lead author Rodrigo Ferrer-Chavez, a graduate student at Northwestern University in the lab of astronomer Jason Wang, who is also a co-author of the paper. "As you go through this iterative process of trying to find what the telescope actually is doing during observations, you basically see the glare of the star disappearing in very bizarre ways. It gets dimmer and very deformed until at the end you get a pretty clean image and, if you're lucky, there's a blob there, which is a glowing planet."

A Failed Experiment Led to a Major Breakthrough

The idea behind FLIP began taking shape when Ferrer-Chavez was an undergraduate student at the Autonomous University of Yucatán in Mexico. He had already participated in a Summer Undergraduate Research Fellowship project with Wang, who was then a 51 Pegasi b Postdoctoral Fellow (named after the first exoplanet discovered orbiting a Sun-like star) in the laboratory of Dimitri Mawet.

Mawet is the David Morrisroe Professor of Astronomy at Caltech and a senior research scientist at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory, which is managed by Caltech. He also serves as director of instrumentation for the Caltech Optical Observatories.

At the time, Ferrer-Chavez was looking for additional opportunities to pursue research. Meanwhile, Wang had been discussing a potential collaboration with Bouman that would combine her expertise in computational imaging with his knowledge of astronomical instruments. Their shared goal was to improve the ability of telescopes to directly image exoplanets.

The two researchers brought Ferrer-Chavez into the project and began mentoring him. However, the initial effort did not produce the results they had hoped for.

"Rodrigo's senior thesis was an exploration of possible approaches, none of which ended up being what we did for this paper, but it helped rule out what not to do and also helped us understand the problem better," Wang says. "I believe our recent success was only possible because we did that exercise first."

Ferrer-Chavez originally attempted to train machine learning models to predict the patterns of starlight appearing in telescope data. That strategy proved unsuccessful, prompting the team to rethink how the technology could be used.

Instead of asking artificial intelligence to predict what the glare should look like, they turned to computational tools developed for machine learning to perform detailed physical calculations.

These methods allowed the researchers to adjust millions of variables, much like turning an enormous collection of control knobs. Each adjustment helped refine their simulation of the telescope until it more closely reproduced the actual observations. Performing this many calculations manually would be impractical.

The resulting approach combines the physical laws governing light and telescope optics with the computational power of modern optimization methods. Rather than simply guessing what should be removed from an image, FLIP uses a model of how the telescope produces that image in the first place.

James Webb's Archives May Contain More Hidden Worlds

One of the most exciting possibilities is that astronomers may not need new telescope observations to benefit from the discovery.

Webb has already collected enormous amounts of astronomical data, including images of stars that could harbor planets too faint to detect using earlier analysis methods. FLIP offers a way to revisit these observations and search for objects that were overlooked.

"We now have a tool that lets us discover and study planets that we were not able to 'see' before, even if that planet was already in an image in the JWST archive," Ferrer-Chavez explains. "We can try to find planets that may have been missed there, and also planets that perhaps we tried to image, but we could not actually detect at the time."

This means some planets that were previously considered beyond the reach of direct imaging could potentially be identified without sending another telescope into space.

It also changes how astronomers can think about existing observations. An image that appears to contain nothing but a brilliant star may hold additional information about planets hidden beneath its glare.

Pushing Telescopes Beyond Their Physical Limits

Astronomers have spent decades developing technology to reduce the overwhelming brightness of stars. One important tool is a coronagraph, an instrument designed to block starlight so that faint objects nearby can be observed more clearly.

For example, NASA's recently launched Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope carries a Coronagraph Instrument developed by researchers at Caltech, JPL, and IPAC (a science and data center for astronomy at Caltech). The instrument is specifically designed to suppress starlight and improve the direct imaging of exoplanets.

But even exceptionally precise telescope hardware has unavoidable limitations. Wang explains that physical instruments alone cannot match the level of correction achieved by FLIP when processing the images.

"The James Webb Space Telescope, which we demonstrated on, is optically stable to the nanometer level, which just sounds pretty awesome, but that's still limiting," he says. "We're excited because this technique is not limited specifically to JWST. Because of some very specific design, this technique could be used on any telescope."

A nanometer is one billionth of a meter, meaning Webb's optical system maintains extraordinary precision. Yet even disturbances on this incredibly small scale can interfere with the detection of planets next to bright stars.

FLIP offers a different way around that problem. Instead of requiring the telescope itself to eliminate every tiny imperfection, the technique mathematically accounts for those effects after the observations have been made.

The researchers now intend to refine FLIP and investigate how it could work with additional instruments, including the Roman Space Telescope and telescopes operating from Earth's surface.

A Powerful New Tool in the Search for Another Earth

The implications may extend well beyond today's observatories. The researchers believe their approach could help shape future space missions, particularly those designed to search for planets resembling Earth.

One major possibility is NASA's proposed Habitable Worlds Observatory (HWO). The mission is intended to become the first telescope specifically designed to search for evidence of life on planets orbiting other stars.

A central objective would be to photograph Earth-like planets within the habitable zones of stars similar to our Sun. A habitable zone is the range of distances around a star where temperatures could allow liquid water to exist on a planet's surface, assuming suitable atmospheric conditions.

But the technical challenge is enormous. An Earth-like planet orbiting a Sun-like star can be approximately 10 billion times fainter than the star itself, making the planet extraordinarily difficult to distinguish from the surrounding light.

"The goal of HWO is to find Earth-like planets and the habitable zones of Sun-like stars through a direct imaging method," Ferrer-Chavez says. "The engineering challenge of trying to image something that is around 10 billion times fainter than something right next to it is a brutal -- not just to clean the image but to build a telescope that can take that image."

FLIP could make that challenge more manageable. If astronomers can digitally remove more unwanted starlight after capturing an image, future telescope designs may not need to overcome every source of optical instability through hardware alone.

The approach could therefore give engineers greater flexibility while allowing astronomers to extract more information from the light their instruments collect.

"I feel we just unlocked a new frontier," Wang says. "We've now gone beyond the telescope stability limit for the first time with this new technique, and this new paradigm, I think, can be applied to every instrument that's trying to image exoplanets."

Bringing the Search for Alien Life Into Focus

For Bouman, the long-term ambition is much larger than simply finding more distant planets. Astronomers ultimately hope to obtain direct images of worlds resembling Earth and examine them for possible signs of life.

Reaching that goal will require major advances in telescope engineering, astronomical instruments, and image processing. But the new research demonstrates that some of the most important improvements may come from better ways of interpreting data rather than building increasingly precise hardware.

"There's still a long way to go, but this work shows how much more is possible when we combine advances in telescope technology with computational imaging," Bouman says. "Sometimes, the key to seeing something new isn't just building a better instrument but finding a better way to make sense of the light it collects."

The findings were published in Nature in a paper titled "Imaging exoplanets below the telescope stability limit." Additional co-authors include William T. Freeman and Brandon Y. Feng of MIT, along with Laurent Pueyo and Marshall D. Perrin of the Space Telescope Science Institute in Baltimore.

The research received support from the Space Telescope Science Institute, the Sloan Foundation, an Amazon AI4Science Discovery Award, and the National Science Foundation.

#606. Mediums new app tries to save the platform from irrelevance

A personal essay, a tutorial, or an argument that's been knocking around in someone's head for weeks can all find a home on Medium. Now the platform wants to be part of what happens before those pieces are ready to publish — when a writer is still sorting through notes or sending an early draft to a friend.

On Nov. 9, Medium launched TK, a free writing and note-taking app for people with a Medium account. TK has an AI assistant built into the editor that can answer questions or give feedback as writers draft. It also lets writers share work in small groups where others can leave comments, swap notes, or contribute writing of their own.

It's goal, according to CEO of Medium Tony Stubblebine, is to "craft writing that compounds into deep thinking, which creates influence across your entire world. What shape is that influence? We’re still figuring that out, but we know it starts with humans writing more."

"We’ve designed TK for three goals: Easy to write. Easy to think. Easy to feel like your writing matters," he added.

The launch comes as the internet has changed. Medium relies on Google to send readers to its articles (44 percent of their traffic, specifically), and Stubblebine said those referrals declined "When Google started taking away all the traffic." He warned the company in an interview with The Verge that "this hurts us, but it also hurts you." Though Medium is profitable since 2024, iterating must be done to compete with both other publishing platforms (like Substack) and social media.

Stubblebine's response is to give writers a way to reach readers without relying on Google. With TK, they can share drafts directly with people in private groups. 'We’re just going to take everything underground," he told The Verge. Beyond Medium, in an analysis of March 2025 browsing data, Pew Research Center found that users clicked a traditional search result on 8 percent of Google visits with an AI summary, compared with 15 percent of visits without one.

For those who don't see the letters everyday (like me), "TK" is used by writers and editors to mark something in their work that's still to come, like a date that needs checking or a quote that needs dropping in. In Medium’s app, typing @TK calls up an assistant that can answer a quick question or find a paragraph the writer saved the day before. Medium says the assistant can also give headline and editorial feedback, compare drafts with Medium publications' style guides, and pull related notes into summaries that link back to the originals. Writers can import their Medium archives or bring over notes from Bear and Obsidian. An optional Claude integration lets an outside agent work with the notebook, too.

The human version of that feedback happens in what TK calls Libraries. A Library can be a space for a few friends to share work in progress, and Medium is also opening groups for journaling, getting feedback on drafts, and working toward a self-published book. A draft can stay private, go to a handful of readers, or eventually make its way to Medium's publishing platform. According to Stubblebine, "TK is the gym and Medium is the stage."

That division makes for an awkward question: The company wants people to do more of their own writing while putting AI a few keystrokes away from every unfinished sentence. Looking up a date is one thing, but asking AI for feedback on a paragraph can get a lot closer to outsourcing writing abilities. Medium adds that TK lets writers disclose which material came from AI and show the prompts and source material behind it, and maintains the service is not anti-AI, but pro-human:

Other publishing platforms are finding uses for AI, too. And as Mashable reported in July, Substack also gives readers "Scan for AI text" option. The tool, powered by Pangram, estimates how much of a published post was written by a human, generated by AI, or written with AI assistance.

The app comes in a literary world that has been inundated with AI. In a 2026 survey Medium conducted with The Harris Poll, 71 percent of writers said they'd used AI in their writing process in the previous three months, yet 69 percent said they distrusted AI-assisted writing from others. TK brings AI closer into the writing process than ever before. Having an assistant a few keystrokes away could make it easier to churn out more AI slop...or the app could do what Stubblebine hopes and get writers sharing rough drafts and working through ideas together. As someone who reads a lot of AI slop these days, I'll be hoping for the latter.

#604. After 40 years, CERN finds evidence of a one-in-10-billion particle decay

After more than 40 years of searching, physicists at CERN have reached a major milestone in their hunt for one of the rarest known particle decays. Researchers working with the LHCb experiment have detected evidence of a process expected to occur only once in approximately 10 billion beauty particle decays.

The finding, announced during a seminar at CERN, marks the first time a single experiment has produced sufficient evidence for this extraordinarily rare event. Beyond its rarity, the decay is particularly valuable because it could help scientists investigate particles that are too massive to be created directly in the Large Hadron Collider.

At the center of the discovery is a particle called Bd, which contains a beauty quark and a down antiquark. Scientists found evidence that this particle can decay into a pair of muons (μ+μ-). Muons are similar to electrons but have greater mass. The results agree with predictions from the Standard Model, the leading theory describing fundamental particles and their interactions, and help narrow the possibilities for new particles beyond it.

Why Rare Particle Decays Could Reveal New Physics

One reason physicists are so interested in these unusual decays is that quantum effects allow other particles to influence how they occur. Even particles too heavy to be produced in the Large Hadron Collider can potentially leave subtle traces in the rates of these processes.

By measuring extremely rare decays with high precision, researchers can look for differences between experimental results and theoretical predictions. Such discrepancies could provide indirect evidence of previously unknown particles. Generally, the rarer a decay is, the more sensitive it can be to these hidden influences.

The decay known as Bd → μ+μ-belongs to a special category that physicists have long considered 'golden channels' for investigating physics beyond the Standard Model. During this process, a beauty quark and an antiquark with the opposite electric charge annihilate each other. Their energy is transformed into a muon and its antiparticle.

Scientists have previously observed a related process involving a different beauty particle, called Bs. In 2015, a joint analysis by the LHCb and CMS collaborations reported the first confirmed observation of Bs → μ+μ-, along with initial evidence for the much rarer Bd decay.

However, subsequent analyses incorporating data collected through 2018 could not confirm the earlier evidence for Bd → μ+μ-. The difference in rarity is substantial. The Bs decay occurs approximately three times per billion beauty particle decays, while the Bd process is predicted to happen about 30 times less frequently.

Upgraded CERN Detector Makes the Breakthrough Possible

A significant improvement to the LHCb experiment helped researchers overcome the enormous difficulty of detecting such a rare event.

Following a major upgrade, the experiment began collecting data in 2024 at five times the rate achieved between 2010 and 2018. The upgraded detector also became better at identifying different kinds of particles, giving researchers a clearer picture of the activity produced during collisions.

The team combined these improvements with machine learning techniques to distinguish the exceptionally rare signals from the vast number of other particle processes. Without these advances, the events scientists wanted to study would have been overwhelmed by the background.

"Physicists have been chasing this decay for more than forty years," said Marco Santimaria, who co-led the international analysis team of 19 scientists. "Extreme precision is required to pick the muons out of the overwhelming background."

Four Decades of Searching Finally Produce Strong Evidence

In particle physics, scientists use a statistical measure called standard deviations to evaluate how likely an apparent signal is to result from random fluctuations rather than a genuine physical process.

A result must reach three standard deviations to qualify as evidence of a new phenomenon. At that level, the probability of random fluctuations producing a signal at least as statistically extreme is approximately 0.3%.

Using data collected during 2024 and 2025, the LHCb researchers identified evidence of Bd → μ+μ- at a significance of 3.0 standard deviations. When they combined those measurements with earlier data gathered between 2011 and 2018, the statistical significance increased to 3.2 standard deviations, exceeding the widely accepted threshold for evidence.

The same investigation also produced the most precise measurements ever obtained for the related Bs → μ+μ-decay, providing additional information about these exceptionally uncommon processes.

Although the new findings represent a major achievement, physicists have not yet reached the threshold required to declare a confirmed observation. That milestone requires a significance of five standard deviations.

If future measurements achieve that level of certainty, Bd → μ+μ-would become the rarest beauty particle decay ever observed.

Future Large Hadron Collider Upgrades Could Uncover Hidden Particles

Researchers expect upcoming improvements to the Large Hadron Collider to make these measurements even more powerful.

The High Luminosity LHC, also known as the HiLumi LHC, is scheduled to begin operating in 2030. Another major upgrade to the LHCb experiment is planned for the middle of the following decade. Together, these developments could eventually increase the experiment's data collection rate by another factor of ten.

Collecting much larger datasets would allow physicists to detect more of these extremely rare decays and measure their properties with greater precision. That increased sensitivity could make it possible to identify subtle effects associated with particles that remain beyond the direct reach of existing accelerators.

"Seeing evidence of this rare decay is incredibly exciting, opening up new possibilities to probe beyond the direct reach of the LHC," explained Harry Cliff, LHCb's Rare Decays Convener and Analysis Co-lead.

"The future LHCb upgrade will allow us to observe many more of these decays, providing even greater sensitivity to undiscovered particles."

For physicists who have pursued this elusive signal for more than four decades, the new evidence represents a long-awaited advance. It also offers a promising way to investigate whether the known laws of particle physics are part of a much larger picture.
