#603. What is Muse Ember? Meet Meta’s new low-cost AI model

Booking a vacation sounds like one thing to hand off to an AI assistant, right up until it has to check the calendar, open a dozen tabs, and figure out whether the cheapest flight is still the cheapest once a carry-on is involved. Every one of those steps takes work, and when an AI agent is doing it, the cost can add up.

Meta has a new model built for that part of the job.

On Nov. 9, Meta's Superintelligence Labs introduced Muse Ember, which the company calls its smallest and most cost-efficient AI model yet. It's designed for the steps an agent might repeat dozens of times in a single assignment, like pulling details out of a document, calling another tool, or running a test after changing a line of code.

Muse is the app with the cute little avatar — named Jolly — that Meta has been promoting since September. The AI assistant can work across (some) apps with a user's permission. The app passed 5 million downloads in 22 days, and has already had many updates; last week, Meta started rolling out voice conversations and animated avatars for the assistant in the U.S. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Meta also uses the Muse name for a family of AI models. The company positions Muse Wonder as the model for its toughest problems and Muse Spark as a model for coding and other work that involves using tools. Ember is the cheaper option for repeatable tasks. Meta's pitch is that developers can use Wonder to plan a complicated job and hand off the individual steps to Ember. For now, Meta lists Ember as available through its Muse Code tool and developer API.

In early testing shared by Meta, cloud storage company Box said it used Ember to check contracts clause by clause against a company’s negotiation rules and assign an overall risk rating. Meta says the model can take in up to a million tokens at once, enough to work through large sets of documents, and can handle images, audio, and video alongside text. That gives developers room to build an agent that can read a scanned contract or pull details from a meeting recording.

The price is what makes Ember interesting. On Artificial Analysis's benchmark, Ember and Anthropic's Claude Haiku 5.5 both rounded to a score of 36 at their highest reasoning settings. Ember cost about 14 cents per benchmark task, compared with 41 cents for Haiku, even though the two have the same standard rates of 10 cents per million input tokens and 50 cents per million output tokens. OpenAI's GPT-6 Luna scored 28 and cost about 15 cents per task at its highest setting.

In Meta's published results, Ember beat Haiku on a terminal coding test, while Haiku did better on a test that judged whether code changes were ready to merge. Meta added that it already has even cheaper models in training.

As the company continues to bet that AI assistants will take on more everyday tasks, developers will need an affordable way to handle all the little steps behind the scenes. A flight search, for example, can mean sorting through dozens of fares, each with its own rules. If Meta wants people to hand off that kind of chore every day, it has to make checking the carry-on policy cheap on its end, too.

#601. Waymo Said It Fixed Its School Bus Problem. The US Government Says It Didn’t Work

The US government is examining whether Waymo’s recall of software involved with a series of school bus passing incidents last fall actually fixed the safety issue.

The formal investigation by the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration will determine whether the company’s supposed software fix succeeded in preventing its robotaxis from improperly passing stopped school buses with red lights on and their stop arms extended—indicators that the buses were picking up and dropping off students. It is illegal to pass these stopped school buses in all 50 states.

According to a filing, Waymo issued a fix for the software issue in mid-November and a voluntary recall a month later. But the government says that the majority of school bus issues occurred after that date.

Earlier this year, a report from the National Transportation Safety Board, the US’s independent safety agency, found that another school bus passing incident occurred in mid-January, several weeks after the supposed software fix. The NTSB is also investigating the school bus passing issue.

In a written statement, Waymo spokesperson Dana Gallagher said the company has “made significant improvements to vehicle behavior around school buses” and that it has not been cited for passing school buses since March—months after the mid-December software recall. She wrote that Waymo looks “forward to continuing [its] engagement on recall processes for AV software that continues to learn and improve.”

Late last year, WIRED reported, the Austin, Texas, school district even helped set up a half-day data collection event for Waymo’s vehicles, coordinating with the self-driving vehicle developer to bring school buses and stop-arm signals to an empty parking lot so that the company could collect information to help train its vehicles to avoid the illegal street maneuver. The data collection event occurred after Waymo had issued its software fix and voluntary recall. Still, the incidents continued.

In years past, recall queries like this one have led to civil penalties, some of them costing automakers and software developers tens of millions of dollars.

#599. NASA reveals bold new plan to return astronauts to the Moon in 2028

NASA has revealed new details about its accelerated effort to return American astronauts to the Moon in 2028. The updated strategy includes testing two competing lunar landers, streamlining rocket preparations, and introducing a lighter spacesuit to help make the first moonwalks possible sooner.

During an October 23 briefing, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman and other agency leaders outlined the next steps for the Artemis program. The revised approach centers on two major missions: Artemis III, a test flight scheduled for 2027, and Artemis IV, which is intended to land astronauts on the lunar surface in 2028.

The announcement builds on priorities introduced earlier this year through NASA's Ignition initiative. Agency officials also highlighted recent progress in spacecraft development, mission preparations, and partnerships with private aerospace companies. These efforts are intended to reduce technical risks, accelerate the schedule, and establish the foundation for a lasting American presence on the Moon.

"This is a very energized NASA, and these updates are only a small part of that story. We are bringing extreme focus to the mission to ensure delivery of world-changing outcomes as quickly, safely, affordably, and successfully as possible," said Isaacman. "NASA is driving outcomes to make Moon missions a reality, and we're not waiting for others to bring their plans to us -- the campaign must move with urgency as strategic competitors pursue a permanent presence beyond Earth."

Artemis III Will Test Two Lunar Landers in Earth Orbit

Before attempting another crewed Moon landing, NASA plans to conduct a major rehearsal in Earth orbit. The Artemis III mission will bring together NASA's Orion spacecraft and experimental lunar landers developed by SpaceX and Blue Origin.

Four astronauts will travel aboard Orion, launching on NASA's powerful SLS (Space Launch System) rocket. During the planned 14-day mission, the crew will practice rendezvous and docking procedures with both companies' lander test vehicles. Rendezvous involves guiding two spacecraft to meet in orbit, while docking allows them to connect so astronauts can move between vehicles.

These demonstrations are designed to verify important spacecraft capabilities before Artemis IV attempts to carry astronauts to the Moon's surface.

The tests will take place in a carefully planned sequence. Blue Origin will first launch a test version of its Blue Moon lander into Earth orbit. NASA will then send Orion and its four astronauts into space aboard the SLS rocket, where Orion will rendezvous and dock with the Blue Moon vehicle.

Next, SpaceX will launch a test version of its Version 3 Starship lunar lander. Orion will separate from Blue Moon and perform another docking maneuver, this time with Starship. By testing both systems during a single crewed mission, NASA aims to gather essential operational experience while reducing uncertainties before the 2028 landing attempt.

NASA Cuts Rocket Preparation Times and Costs

The new mission details follow other recent Artemis progress reports. According to NASA, the hardware needed for Artemis III remains on schedule, and improvements in processing have reduced preparation timelines by approximately 50%.

The agency also reported a significant engineering achievement involving a critical rocket fuel-line component. Rather than relying on an outside supplier, NASA engineers redesigned, manufactured, and tested the component themselves in just five months. The work cost roughly one-tenth of an earlier estimate from an external vendor.

"NASA should be expected to do this kind of work, and we have some of the best technical knowledge in the world on rocket hardware and operations to do it," said Jeremy Parsons, the agency's Artemis program manager. "It's an example of how we are rebuilding our core competencies and developing centers of excellence in technical functions instead of continuing to outsource expertise."

The improvements reflect NASA's broader effort to strengthen its internal engineering capabilities, shorten development schedules, and reduce the cost of preparing missions for launch.

SpaceX and Blue Origin Offer Different Paths to the Moon

For Artemis IV in 2028, NASA intends to launch astronauts aboard Orion using the SLS rocket from Kennedy Space Center in Florida. Once in space, the crew will rely on a lunar landing system developed by either SpaceX or Blue Origin to reach the Moon's surface.

Both companies have revised their original mission designs to make operations simpler, more efficient, and more adaptable. Although they share the same objective, their approaches to transporting astronauts to the lunar surface differ considerably.

SpaceX plans to use its Starship spacecraft, which will take on additional propellant while orbiting Earth. After refueling, Starship will meet Orion and carry the spacecraft toward the Moon. Refueling in orbit is an important part of SpaceX's strategy because it allows the vehicle to receive the propellant needed for its journey after leaving the launchpad.

Blue Origin is developing a different approach using its Blue Moon Mark 2 lander. Its plan calls for additional propulsion stages in Earth orbit to send the lander toward the Moon. The lander will then meet Orion in a relatively low lunar orbit before carrying astronauts down to the surface.

Both companies are working toward having their systems ready to support a crewed Moon landing in 2028. Maintaining two potential landing systems gives NASA greater flexibility as development and testing continue.

NASA Explores New Rocket Options for Artemis IV

NASA is also considering two possible upper stages for the SLS rocket used on Artemis IV. An upper stage is the portion of a rocket that provides additional propulsion after the earlier stages have completed their work, helping send a spacecraft toward its destination.

One option is the interim cryogenic propulsion stage already used by SLS. The other is an extended version of United Launch Alliance's Centaur V upper stage, which would hold additional propellant.

By developing both possibilities, NASA hopes to preserve flexibility in its launch plans and avoid becoming dependent on a single technical solution. Having alternatives could help the agency respond to engineering challenges without jeopardizing the broader Moon landing schedule.

A New Lightweight Spacesuit Could Speed Up Moonwalks

Another important change involves the spacesuits astronauts will wear while exploring the lunar surface.

NASA is working with Axiom Space on a second, lighter design known as the "Sortie suit." The new suit is intended specifically for the first Moon surface excursions and weighs approximately half as much as Axiom's larger primary spacesuit.

The lighter design could simplify preparations for the earliest landing missions and help astronauts begin exploring the lunar surface sooner.

However, the Sortie suit is not intended to replace the primary design. Axiom's more capable suit will remain part of NASA's plans for future missions that require longer periods outside the spacecraft and more demanding surface activities.

Building a Permanent Human Presence on the Moon

NASA's ambitions extend well beyond returning astronauts to the lunar surface for a single mission.

As the Artemis program advances, the agency intends to send crews on increasingly challenging expeditions near the Moon's South Pole. This region is an important focus of future lunar exploration, and the missions are designed to expand how much of the Moon humans can investigate directly.

NASA describes the effort as part of a new Golden Age of innovation and exploration. The agency expects its lunar missions to support scientific discoveries, generate economic benefits, and encourage the development of technologies needed for more ambitious space exploration.

Ultimately, the goal is to establish an enduring human presence on the Moon rather than repeat the brief visits of earlier lunar expeditions. The experience gained from operating spacecraft, supporting astronauts, and carrying out increasingly complex missions on the lunar surface will also help NASA prepare for another historic objective: sending the first astronauts to Mars.

SpaceX is one big step closer to the 15th test flight of its Starship megarocket.

The company announced today (Nov. 10) that it had moved its latest Starship first stage, known as Super Heavy Booster 22, to the launch mount at its Starbase facility in South Texas ahead of Flight 15, which is scheduled to lift off this Friday (Nov. 13).

During the upcoming launch test, SpaceX plans to catch both Booster 22 and Starship's Ship upper stage with its launch tower's "chopstick" arms in a double landing catch — something the private spaceflight company has never tried before. It will be the first full recovery of an orbital rocket in history.

Booster 22 therefore "was used for final catch testing upon arrival [at the pad], including a suspended squeeze test at catch height," SpaceX said in an X post today (Nov. 10).

That post shared four photos of the giant booster, which is powered by 33 of SpaceX's next-gen Raptor engines. The first shot showed Super Heavy in one of Starbase's "Mega Bay" assembly buildings before rollout, while the other three depicted it grasped by the tower's arms, held high in the South Texas sky.

SpaceX has used the chopstick arms during Starship flights before; it has successfully caught Super Heavy on three separate occasions. But the company has never attempted a catch of Ship, which flies higher and farther than the booster and is therefore a more challenging target. And SpaceX only reached orbit with Starship for the first time on Flight 14, which launched on Sept. 28.

So Flight 15 will break new ground. And, if all goes to plan, it will give us a look at the future: SpaceX plans to catch Super Heavy and Ship on all operational Starship missions, as this recovery strategy will allow the company to boost the megarocket's launch cadence.

And SpaceX wants to fly Starship incredibly often, so that it can build out its Starlink V3 broadband constellation and even bigger Starmind AI data-center network. Indeed, Elon Musk has said the company is eyeing 10,000 Starship flights per year by 2030.

If all goes to plan, Flight 15 will launch from Starbase on Friday during a 75-minute window that opens at 5:45 p.m. EST (2245 GMT; 4:45 p.m. local Texas time).

Super Heavy will return to Starbase for its catch about 6.5 minutes after liftoff. Ship will follow suit much later — about 22 hours later, in fact, after it has deployed 26 Starlink V3 spacecraft into LEO and completed 14 circuits of our planet.

#595. Scientists finally discover why we learn to love bitter coffee

A first taste of black coffee can be surprisingly unpleasant. Its sharp bitterness is the kind of flavor our brains are naturally inclined to avoid. Yet millions of people eventually come to enjoy that same taste, often looking forward to their morning cup. What changes?

Scientists at UC San Francisco may have uncovered an important part of the answer. Their research reveals a little-understood dopamine pathway linking the digestive system and the brain, helping explain how we develop a liking for certain flavors through repeated experience.

Dopamine is a chemical messenger involved in motivation, reward, and learning. Researchers have long known that it helps the brain recognize experiences worth repeating. However, most studies have examined rapid dopamine signals that appear within seconds of encountering something rewarding, such as the sight of a favorite meal.

The new research, published November 4 in Nature, examines a different system. This slower pathway responds to information coming from inside the body, allowing the brain to learn about the benefits of food after it has been consumed.

"We've shown that this slow dopamine system is what drives us to learn to like certain foods over time, and we can see where and how that happens," said Zachary Knight, PhD, professor of Physiology and senior author of the study.

The findings offer a possible explanation for why an initially unpleasant flavor can gradually become enjoyable. Once the brain connects the taste of coffee with caffeine's stimulating effects, Knight explained, "that coffee starts to taste a lot better."

How the Gut Sends Reward Signals to the Brain

Scientists have spent decades investigating dopamine and its influence on behavior. Its familiar, rapidly acting signals help us respond to opportunities for reward. But dopamine also operates on a slower timescale, through a system that has received far less attention.

According to Knight, this slower process largely unfolds outside our conscious awareness.

When food enters the digestive tract, specialized sensors detect nutrients, including sugar and fat. Information about those nutrients travels toward the brain, where it can produce a dopamine response that develops gradually and continues for several minutes.

To investigate this process, Knight worked with James Grove, PhD, who was a graduate student at the time. They introduced nutrients directly into the stomachs of mice while monitoring dopamine activity throughout their brains.

The researchers used either sugar water or a liquid resembling a milkshake. Delivering these substances directly into the stomach allowed them to separate the effects of nutrients from the experience of tasting food.

The results revealed a powerful, prolonged dopamine response in a specific brain region called the anterior basolateral amygdala, or BLA.

The amygdala is involved in emotional processing and learning, including forming associations between experiences and their consequences. Scientists already knew that dopamine was released in this particular region, but its purpose had remained unclear.

"We could see that this dopamine is part of a different system and begin to understand its role," he said.

Scientists Find a Brain Circuit That Changes Food Preferences

Finding the dopamine signal was only the beginning. The researchers next wanted to determine whether it actually influenced how animals learned to prefer different flavors.

They first interrupted the newly identified pathway in mice. The animals continued eating normally, indicating that the disruption did not prevent them from consuming food. However, they lost the ability to learn that an unfamiliar flavor was associated with receiving calories.

The team then conducted the opposite experiment.

After mice tasted a flavor containing no calories, the researchers artificially activated dopamine fibers in the BLA. Remarkably, the animals developed a preference for that flavor even though it provided no nutritional reward.

In effect, stimulating the pathway reproduced the learning signal that nutrients would ordinarily generate.

Together, the experiments showed that this dopamine system plays a critical role in teaching the brain which flavors predict rewarding nutritional effects.

A Surprising Discovery in the Left Side of the Brain

The researchers also looked for evidence that a comparable process occurs in humans. Brain imaging studies conducted by Knight's collaborators revealed a strikingly similar pattern.

Dana Small, PhD, a psychologist at Yale University, revisited data from an earlier positron emission tomography (PET) study involving 13 men. Participants had consumed either a milkshake or a drink without calories.

Her analysis found that dopamine release following nutrient delivery was especially concentrated in the left side of the BLA.

A separate study led by Marc Tittgemeyer, PhD, and his colleagues provided additional evidence. Using functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI), which measures changes in brain activity associated with blood flow, the researchers examined 38 adults after they consumed glucose, a simple sugar.

They observed increased activity in the same brain region.

One of the most unexpected findings was that the response was not evenly distributed between the brain's two hemispheres.

In both mice and humans, nutrients produced a considerably stronger dopamine response in the left BLA than in the corresponding region on the right.

That imbalance surprised Knight because brain networks responsible for essential functions, such as feeding, are generally expected to work in a relatively symmetrical fashion.

Why the left side plays such a prominent role remains unanswered. Knight's laboratory is now investigating what causes this unusual difference.

Could This Discovery Help Scientists Change Food Cravings?

Identifying the pathway provides researchers with a clearer picture of how the digestive system influences the brain's learned responses to food. It also raises an intriguing possibility: Could scientists eventually use this knowledge to change which foods people find appealing?

One area of interest involves GLP-1 medications, a class of drugs that mimic a hormone naturally released by the gut. These medications can substantially alter appetite and eating behavior.

Knight's team did not investigate GLP-1 drugs in the new study, so it remains unknown whether they influence the dopamine pathway the researchers identified.

Still, Knight suggests that one possible explanation for some of their effects could involve reducing the dopamine signals generated when nutrients reach the digestive system. If that happens, foods might gradually become less rewarding because the brain would receive weaker signals reinforcing their flavors.

For now, this remains a hypothesis rather than a demonstrated effect of the medications.

The broader implications extend beyond hunger and fullness. Many approaches to changing eating behavior focus on reducing appetite or increasing feelings of satisfaction after a meal. The newly mapped pathway suggests that the brain's process of learning food preferences could eventually become another target.

By influencing how the brain connects flavors with their consequences, future treatments might someday help people develop different preferences rather than simply making them feel less hungry.

"By understanding the steps between nutrient detection in the gut and learning in the brain, it might be possible to fine-tune them to subtly change our preferences for different foods," Knight said.

The discovery offers a new perspective on why our tastes can change over time. From learning to enjoy bitter coffee to developing a preference for calorie-rich foods, what we love to eat may depend partly on a quiet conversation between the gut and brain that continues long after the first bite.

The research was supported by the National Institutes of Health (grants R01-DK106399, R01-DK138127, R01-NS116626, and R01-DK145100 and F31-NS120468).

#594. Jensen Huang’s Troubles Leave the AI Industry Reeling

No executive has pulled the strings in the artificial intelligence era quite like Nvidia’s CEO, Jensen Huang. He parlayed the boom into incredible wealth, became a close confidant of President Donald Trump, and emerged as a prominent voice in US–China relations. That now all stands in the balance.

Over the past few days, Huang’s status as an AI titan has come under scrutiny amid an admission that he had an affair with Alice Huang, an executive at a Singapore-based company whom US intelligence services concluded was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia’s board of directors said the company would launch an investigation into the incident. Huang, the company said, “fully supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it.” Huang earned $36 million last year and owned about 3.5 percent of Nvidia as of March, according to a corporate filing—which also noted that Huang is an at-will employee who can be let go anytime without severance.

How exactly the investigation shakes out, and what it means for Huang’s future at the company, remains to be seen. But the incident at least puts his position at risk, and raises questions about how the industry will respond.

“He’s the person who decides who gets the chips and who doesn't,” says David Yoffie, a professor at Harvard Business School who tracks the chip industry.

Nvidia, which Huang cofounded in 1993, controls 90 percent of the market for chips used in training frontier AI models. With those chips in short supply, Huang has the power to determine which areas of AI get a boost next. Nvidia has also bankrolled much of the AI buildout, investing billions in the very companies buying its chips, such as OpenAI and Anthropic. When Elon Musk wants to build a new data center, Yoffie says, he can put in a call to Huang.

Internally, Huang closely manages even small details at Nvidia, responds to emails from any one of its roughly 60,000 staffers, and raises his voice to get points across, according to current and former employees. He’s a regular on cable TV and at tech conferences, where he’s been trying to deflate concerns about an AI market bubble and boost the positioning of his company.

But Nvidia has no other executive anywhere close to Huang’s prominence, and no apparent potential successor. “We have no idea who would replace him,” Yoffie says. “You put someone new into that position, if you're a customer, who knows what that's going to mean.”

Ryan Fedasiuk, a senior fellow at the think tank Foundation for American Innovation, says Huang has played such an important role—and become so close to the president—that he has become load-bearing for the entire US AI industry.

“On the one hand, Huang is irreplaceable. He built Nvidia into the juggernaut it is today, and he was the visionary behind the GPU, which has sustained the intelligence revolution,” Fedasiuk says. But he says that the company, which is indispensable to the AI industry, has to come first.

“He has to be replaceable as the CEO of the world's largest company, at $4 trillion in capex.”

Nvidia shares fell more than 4 percent on Monday, even as broader stock market indices rose.

The board’s investigation will most likely focus on whether Huang violated any company policies, Yoffie says. But the FBI could also investigate whether laws were broken, and Congress may decide to look into whether Huang’s affair influenced the US government’s policy on selling chips to China.

Nvidia didn’t immediately respond to WIRED’s request for comment.

The scandal also raises serious questions about whether China may have gained sensitive information about US AI companies and the government’s strategy on China, Fedasiuk says.

Huang’s affair appears especially damning given how outspoken he has been in recent years against calls for more aggressive technology export controls on China. His credibility on the topic could be permanently in doubt.

Both the first Trump administration and the Biden administration imposed export controls designed to limit China’s access to Nvidia’s chips, as well as to technology required to mint advanced semiconductors. In his second term, however, Trump loosened these controls, giving China access to older generations of Nvidia chips; it was better to make China reliant on US technology, the argument went.

Sam Bresnick, a research fellow at Georgetown University’s Center for Security and Emerging Technology, says the Huang scandal is likely to accelerate efforts to crack down on chip exports.

“There was going to be more of an impetus to control what chips are getting sent to China anyway,” he says. “I expect there to be more scrutiny on what's going on, what's getting sent to China. I think there's also going to be more scrutiny from the Commerce Department.”

#593. NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 10, 2026

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you talk a good game.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

#592. Alzheimer’s may have early warning signs outside the brain

Alzheimer's disease is best known for its effects on memory and thinking, but new research suggests that some symptoms may develop in a surprising place: outside the brain.

Scientists at the University of Central Florida (UCF) have discovered that genetic mutations associated with Alzheimer's can interfere with the nerves that control muscle movement, even without involvement from the brain or spinal cord. The findings could help explain why some people with Alzheimer's experience changes in walking, balance, or coordination long before noticeable memory problems emerge.

The discovery raises the possibility that movement difficulties could provide earlier warning signs of the disease and that treatments focused exclusively on the brain may overlook important problems elsewhere in the body.

The research was led by James Hickman, a professor at UCF's Nanoscience Technology Center, and Research Professor Xiufang "Nadine" Guo, working alongside scientists at the healthcare technology company Hesperos. Funded by the National Institutes of Health's National Institute on Aging, the study used laboratory systems built from human cells to investigate how inherited Alzheimer's mutations affect movement. The findings were published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association.

"Motor deficits may be an earlier indication [of Alzheimer's]," Guo says. "If we can detect those changes and intervene earlier, that could help delay the onset of central nervous system symptoms."

Why Alzheimer's Symptoms May Appear Before Memory Loss

Alzheimer's is generally associated with progressive memory loss, confusion, and declining cognitive abilities. However, doctors have also observed that some patients develop physical symptoms, including changes in balance, coordination, and gait (manner of walking), years before clear cognitive difficulties become apparent.

These observations have raised an important question: Are such movement problems simply a consequence of damage occurring in the brain, or could Alzheimer's also interfere directly with nerves elsewhere in the body?

To investigate, the UCF team focused on familial Alzheimer's, a rare inherited form of the disease that typically develops earlier in life (from 40 to 65 years of age) than the more common form.

Their experiments revealed that motor neurons carrying mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's could disrupt the connections responsible for controlling muscles, even when the brain and spinal cord were completely absent from the experimental system.

Motor neurons are specialized nerve cells that transmit signals to muscles, telling them when to contract and produce movement. The new findings suggest that Alzheimer's mutations can interfere with this process directly, rather than affecting movement only through damage to the brain.

"This is the first time it's been demonstrated that deficits in the peripheral nervous system can arise directly from these mutations," Hickman says. "It means drugs that target the brain may not fix problems in the rest of the body."

The peripheral nervous system includes the nerves located outside the brain and spinal cord. These nerves carry signals between the central nervous system and the rest of the body, helping control movement, sensation, and other essential functions.

Guo also points out that preserving the ability to move could have benefits beyond physical health. Regular physical activity is associated with better cognitive well-being, so protecting motor function may also help support brain health.

How a Miniature Human Cell System Revealed the Discovery

To examine the relationship between Alzheimer's mutations and muscle movement, the scientists used an innovative laboratory platform known as "human-on-a-chip" technology.

Developed and manufactured through Hesperos, a company co-founded by Hickman, these miniature systems bring different types of human cells together under controlled conditions. They allow researchers to recreate specific biological functions and observe how diseases interfere with them.

Unlike conventional laboratory experiments that examine isolated cells, these systems can reproduce important interactions between different tissues. They also provide an alternative to animal models, which do not always accurately reflect how human diseases behave.

For this study, the team created a miniature model of the neuromuscular junction, the connection where a motor neuron communicates with a muscle cell.

The model was deliberately designed without a brain or spinal cord. This was a crucial part of the experiment because it allowed the researchers to investigate whether Alzheimer's-related changes could cause movement problems independently of the central nervous system.

The scientists combined healthy muscle cells with motor neurons derived from stem cells that carried genetic mutations associated with familial Alzheimer's disease. Stem cells are cells that can develop into specialized types of cells, making them valuable tools for studying human diseases in the laboratory.

The researchers then examined how effectively the altered nerve cells communicated with otherwise healthy muscle tissue.

The results showed that the Alzheimer's-associated mutations could interfere with normal nerve-to-muscle function. This suggests that at least some movement difficulties linked to the disease may arise directly in the peripheral nervous system rather than being caused entirely by degeneration in the brain.

Why Damaged Nerve Connections Can Affect Walking and Balance

Every voluntary movement depends on successful communication between the nervous system and the muscles.

At the neuromuscular junction, an electrical signal traveling along a motor neuron triggers chemical communication that causes a muscle to contract. This process makes everyday activities possible, from walking and maintaining posture to lifting objects and performing precise movements.

When the connection becomes impaired, muscles may respond less effectively to nerve signals. Depending on the severity and location of the problem, this can contribute to weakness, reduced coordination, and difficulty sustaining physical activity.

To evaluate these effects, the research team measured how consistently nerve signals produced muscle contractions and how long the muscles could continue contracting before becoming fatigued.

These measurements provided a direct assessment of how well the nerve and muscle cells worked together. They also resemble aspects of the functional testing physicians use when examining patients with movement disorders.

"You can't move unless the motor circuit works," Hickman says. "When a doctor taps your knee to check your reflex, they're testing that exact connection."

By demonstrating impaired nerve-to-muscle communication in a system containing no brain or spinal cord, the study provides evidence that mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's can produce effects beyond the central nervous system.

However, the experiments do not establish that these changes necessarily occur before memory loss in patients. Further research will be needed to determine how closely the laboratory findings reflect the progression of Alzheimer's in people, particularly those with the more common forms of the disease.

Could This Discovery Lead to Earlier Alzheimer's Detection?

The findings suggest a potentially important new direction for Alzheimer's research: looking beyond memory and other cognitive symptoms when investigating the disease.

If future studies confirm that changes in motor function are detectable early in certain patients, tests of walking, coordination, or nerve-to-muscle activity might eventually help identify disease-related changes before more obvious neurological symptoms develop.

The results also raise questions about treatment. Many Alzheimer's therapies are designed to address processes occurring in the brain, but medications that target the central nervous system may not necessarily correct abnormalities affecting peripheral nerves and muscles.

Understanding these separate effects could help researchers investigate treatments that protect physical function alongside cognitive health.

Human-on-a-Chip Technology Could Transform Disease Research

Beyond the implications for Alzheimer's, the researchers see considerable potential in using human-on-a-chip systems to investigate other diseases and evaluate possible treatments.

Because these platforms contain human cells and reproduce measurable biological activities, they can help scientists observe disease-related changes in conditions that more closely resemble human physiology than some conventional experimental models.

This approach may be particularly useful in drug development, where researchers need to understand not only how a potential treatment affects individual cells but also how it influences interactions between different tissues.

Such systems can also uncover biological effects that may be difficult to detect in animal experiments, potentially helping researchers identify promising therapies and recognize limitations earlier in development.

For Hickman, the progress represents the culmination of three decades of work devoted to understanding disease through more realistic laboratory models.

"These systems let us study disease in a way that's closer to what actually happens in the human body, and that's what we need to develop better treatments," he says.

#590. A New AI Tool From Google DeepMind Proved the Signal Protocol Is Secure

Google DeepMind researchers used agentic AI to mathematically prove, or “formally verify,” a number of the Signal protocol’s key cryptographic guarantees—a landmark development for the widely used cryptography and AI itself.

Conducted as a sort of proof-of-concept project in just four weeks, the work is significant in two ways. It serves as further independent validation of the cryptography that protects massive quantities of the world's communications on platforms like the Signal app, Google Messages, and WhatsApp. And it demonstrates Google DeepMind's formal verification capabilities using a new AI framework called AlphaProof Code.

More broadly, AI-aided formal verification is having a moment, but it is unclear how practical it will be for widespread use and how effective it can be at securing large amounts of code. Proponents of formal verification have long referred to it as a sort of holy grail in software security, because it uses math to check and validate that software is functioning as intended—in other words, that it doesn't have critical vulnerabilities.

Before the AI era, it was incredibly time-consuming for people to describe the characteristics of software they wanted to prove, turn these summaries into formulas, and then develop the proof. Agentic AI can significantly cut down the time of the latter project, enabling analyses like DeepMind's Signal evaluation. But even this analysis was limited in scope—it did not, for example, formally verify the entire Signal mobile app—and DeepMind researchers emphasize that their findings are just one milestone in a larger process.

“We are very excited by this result, but there is a long distance to go before we are able to scale this to all the software of the world, and we want to do that,” Swarat Chaudhuri, a DeepMind research scientist, tells WIRED.

The DeepMind analysis did not find any new vulnerabilities in the portions of the Signal protocol it looked at, but when the researchers used AlphaProof Code on older versions of Signal that contain known and long-fixed vulnerabilities, it correctly identified them and showed that the protocol could not be formally verified. The researchers are releasing the specification and proof they developed for the Signal protocol analysis to aid other formal verification work.

While the speed of the project was notable, the DeepMind researchers point out that they were able to get a head start to a degree because of some past formal verification work that the Signal Foundation has conducted.

For critical services used in high-stakes settings, it would be a dream to be able to formally verify every code base and entire software supply chains. “If you can now verify the correctness of these implementations, then you can rest easy,” says Pushmeet Kohli, chief scientist at Google Cloud and DeepMind's vice president of research. “You can imagine banks, imagine all those other companies who rely on assurances and who worry about bugs who could now say, ‘I can prove these properties.’"

The open question across the tech sector, though, is whether AI-automated formal verification can truly scale—and whether it can do so at a workable cost. DeepMind conducted the Signal analysis free of charge as a research project, and Google declined to share the cost of the analysis. The company also declined to comment on whether it has plans to release a commercial formal verification product or service.

The researchers say that some of their crucial advancements are in developing a “human-AI collaboration process,” as Chaudhuri puts it, that allows agents to contribute and self-correct at almost every phase of the process while still seeking human input and review at key junctures. “We don’t want this sort of vibe world where AI is generating the goals and AI is generating the proofs and basically taking tests that are self-written," Chaudhuri says. “There’s a middle ground.”

#589. NASA’s CHAPEA Mars crew finally emerges after more than a year in isolation

After spending more than a year experiencing what life could be like on Mars, four NASA volunteers have finally stepped outside, marking the end of an ambitious experiment designed to prepare humans for future missions to the Red Planet.

The crew completed their simulated Mars journey on October 31, 2026, emerging into the outside world for the first time in more than a year. Although the volunteers never actually left Earth, their extended mission recreated many of the challenges astronauts could encounter while living and working millions of miles from home.

The experiment was part of NASA's Crew Health and Performance Exploration Analog (CHAPEA) program, which investigates how people adapt to the demanding conditions of extended space exploration.

The four volunteers were Matthew Montgomery (left), James Spicer (middle left), Ross Elder (middle right), and Ellen Ellis (right). Following the completion of their mission, the crew posed together outdoors, celebrating their return from a simulated journey to another planet.

Throughout the experiment, the participants carried out tasks similar to those that future Mars explorers may need to perform. These included operating and testing essential systems, caring for crops, conserving limited supplies, and conducting extravehicular activities.

Extravehicular activities, often called spacewalks, are operations astronauts perform outside their spacecraft or habitat. In a Mars simulation, these activities help researchers examine how crews might prepare for and carry out work beyond the safety of their living quarters.

The volunteers also had to manage communication delays, an important challenge for anyone traveling to Mars. Unlike astronauts aboard the International Space Station, who can communicate with Earth almost instantly, future Mars explorers will experience significant delays because radio signals take time to cross the enormous distance between the two planets.

These delays mean astronauts cannot always depend on immediate guidance from mission control. They must be prepared to solve problems, make decisions, and manage unexpected situations with greater independence.

One of the biggest challenges of sending people to Mars is keeping them healthy, productive, and safe during long periods away from Earth. A crew must be able to manage food, equipment, living conditions, and daily responsibilities while working together in a confined environment.

NASA uses analog missions to explore these challenges before attempting actual journeys into deep space. An analog mission is a realistic simulation that reproduces selected conditions of space exploration while allowing scientists to study how people respond in a controlled setting on Earth.

The CHAPEA program provides an opportunity to investigate how astronauts might handle the practical demands of extended missions, including resource limitations, equipment maintenance, food production, and communication difficulties.

The lessons learned from Montgomery, Spicer, Elder, and Ellis will help NASA plan future human exploration missions. Their experiences are expected to inform the development of NASA's planned Moon Base and preparations for eventual crewed expeditions to Mars.

Although this crew's journey took place entirely on Earth, the knowledge gained could help address some of the challenges humans will face when they finally travel to the Red Planet.

#586. 237-million-year-old fossils reveal a bizarre armored sea amphibian

A remarkable fossil discovery in China has revealed an ancient amphibian that lived entirely in the ocean, something no amphibian alive today is known to do. The unusual animal, named Gerrothorax maritimus, had a broad, flattened body protected by bony armor and was roughly the size of a giant salamander. Researchers believe it spent much of its life on the seafloor, waiting to ambush passing prey.

The discovery comes from an international team at the University of California, Davis, Peking University, and the Geological Museum of Guizhou. Their findings, published in Nature Ecology & Evolution, are based on two exceptionally well-preserved skeletons dating back nearly 237 million years to the Late Triassic period.

Both fossils were uncovered in China's Guizhou Province, in a region that was once submerged beneath an ancient ocean hundreds of miles from major landmasses.

"This area is part of an ecosystem that we've been looking at and trying to reconstruct," said study corresponding author Ryosuke Motani, a professor in the Department of Earth and Planetary Sciences at UC Davis. "We've been mostly studying marine reptiles and the fish from this place, but now we also have this amphibian, and it's a sort of very unusual member of the lineage."

The finding is especially surprising because amphibians are generally unable to survive in seawater. Among the more than 8,000 amphibian species living today, none are known to spend their entire lives in marine environments, and fewer than 2% have any ability to tolerate salt.

Mammals, reptiles, and birds, by comparison, all include species that can live entirely at sea without relying on land for food or fresh water. Until this discovery, scientists had never identified convincing fossil evidence of an amphibian with the same ability.

A Giant Armored Amphibian Built for the Seafloor

Because the two skeletons are so complete, researchers were able to piece together the animal's appearance and develop a clearer picture of how it lived.

Gerrothorax maritimus measured nearly three feet long, but its proportions were far different from those of a modern salamander. Rather than having a long, slender body, it was unusually broad and flattened, with a distinctive disc-shaped form.

Its body was also covered in thick bony armor, which may have helped protect it from large marine predators such as ichthyosaurs.

"If you think about today's amphibians, this animal is unusual because it's big," Motani said. "It's probably, body mass-wise, about the same as the giant salamander, but what's different is that giant salamanders are elongated whereas this animal is really chunky."

The structure of its skull, together with its heavy armor, suggests that this ancient amphibian was an ambush predator. Instead of actively chasing its meals, it likely rested on the seafloor until an unsuspecting animal came within reach.

"They would probably just wait at the bottom and then when prey comes around, they would just suck it in," said Motani, who noted that the hunting strategy resembles that of the wobbegong, a type of carpet shark that lives near the seafloor and captures prey using suction.

How Could an Amphibian Survive in Salt Water?

One of the biggest mysteries surrounding Gerrothorax maritimus is how it managed to survive in an environment that is hostile to nearly all modern amphibians.

The problem comes down largely to amphibian skin. Unlike the relatively waterproof skin of many other animals, amphibian skin allows water and gases to pass through it. This makes it useful for breathing and maintaining hydration, but also leaves amphibians especially vulnerable to the effects of salt water.

"That skin is permeable, and that allows the animals to use it for multiple purposes," said Motani. "They use it for respiration and water intake, so amphibians don't drink or breathe through the mouth because they can do both through the skin."

As a result, modern amphibians generally depend on freshwater habitats or environments close to them. A few species have developed limited salt tolerance, including the crab-eating frog. Even these animals can lose weight when exposed to brackish water and must eventually return to freshwater to recover.

The ancient Gerrothorax maritimus, however, may have evolved a very different solution.

Researchers suggest that its extensive bony armor restricted the movement of water through its skin. This protective covering may have reduced its dependence on absorbing water directly through the skin, allowing the animal to take in seawater through its mouth instead.

Under this proposed mechanism, water would have been absorbed through the digestive tract, while the animal's gills could have helped eliminate excess salt. Gills are known to have been present in members of the Gerrothorax genus, giving the researchers reason to suspect that this species possessed a more fishlike approach to managing its salt balance.

Together, these adaptations may explain how an amphibian managed to occupy an ecological niche that remains inaccessible to its modern relatives.

Fossil Evidence Points to a Fully Marine Lifestyle

The researchers also examined whether Gerrothorax maritimus truly lived in the ocean or whether its remains might have been transported there after death.

Several clues strongly favor a marine lifestyle.

First, the skeletons were recovered from sediments deposited in a deep marine environment. Their remarkable completeness also provides important evidence about where the animals died.

"Carcasses at sea, unless they sink to the bottom right away, tend to dry out and lose limbs prior to sinking to the bottom," said Motani. "But these animals had their fingers preserved, so that means they probably died near where they lived."

The preservation of such delicate skeletal structures suggests that the bodies were not carried long distances before reaching the seafloor.

To investigate further, the team analyzed the fossils' chemical composition, focusing on the relative abundance of different forms of strontium, known as isotopes.

The results closely matched the strontium isotope signatures expected in ocean water during the Late Triassic, providing another independent line of evidence that the animals inhabited a marine environment.

Taken together, the fossil preservation, geological setting, and chemical findings make a compelling case that Gerrothorax maritimus spent its life entirely at sea.

The discovery reveals an unexpected evolutionary path for amphibians, showing that at least one ancient species appears to have overcome the biological limitations that keep its living relatives largely confined to freshwater environments.

The research received funding from the National Natural Science Foundation of China.

Materials provided by University of California - Davis. Original written by Greg Watry. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#582. Why heart attacks can still happen even when bad cholesterol is low

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have uncovered new clues about why some people develop heart disease even after lowering their bad cholesterol to recommended levels. Their findings point to biological processes involving other fat-carrying particles in the blood that may contribute to cardiovascular damage in ways that go beyond cholesterol buildup. By identifying these previously poorly understood mechanisms, the research could help pave the way for more targeted strategies to prevent heart disease and potentially save lives.

Heart disease remains one of the world's leading causes of death. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), cardiovascular diseases kill nearly 17.9 million people worldwide each year, with heart attacks and strokes responsible for more than 80% of those deaths. Coronary heart disease develops when fatty deposits containing cholesterol (cholesterol) build up inside the arteries that supply blood to the heart. If left untreated, the condition can eventually cause a heart attack.

Reducing LDL cholesterol, commonly known as "bad cholesterol," is a proven way to lower the likelihood of a heart attack. However, bringing LDL cholesterol into the recommended range does not eliminate the danger. Some patients continue to develop coronary heart disease despite successful treatment, suggesting that other particles transporting fats through the bloodstream play an important role. Until recently, the biological reasons behind this remaining risk were not well understood.

"For this study, we wanted to understand what happens beyond the accumulation of cholesterol in artery walls, whether different fat-carrying particles can damage the blood vessels differently and why the remaining risk of heart disease still occurs," says Jakub Morze, lead author and a postdoctoral fellow at Chalmers University of Technology, in Sweden.

Cholesterol is a substance similar to fat that circulates in the bloodstream. The body needs it to build cells and manufacture certain vitamins and hormones. Problems can arise when too much cholesterol accumulates in blood vessel walls, creating deposits called plaques.

These plaques can narrow arteries and interfere with blood flow. If a plaque ruptures, a blood clot may form quickly and completely obstruct the affected vessel, potentially triggering a heart attack or stroke.

Because cholesterol and other fats cannot travel through the blood on their own, they are transported by specialized particles known as lipoproteins. Three major classes of these particles contain a surface protein called apolipoprotein B (apoB). When too many of these particles circulate in the blood, they can deliver excess cholesterol into artery walls. The cholesterol they carry is commonly referred to as "bad cholesterol."

To investigate these unanswered questions, the scientists used proteomics analysis to examine how three types of fat-carrying particles (LDL, TRL and Lp(a)) affect the body and may contribute to heart disease. This approach allowed the team to investigate biological changes associated with the particles rather than focusing exclusively on the cholesterol they carry.

All three types can contribute to cholesterol deposits inside artery walls, although LDL, which stands for low-density lipoprotein, is the most common. In a previous study, the same research group found that counting harmful lipoprotein particles was the most effective way to predict future heart disease risk.

The researchers also identified two particularly important contributors beyond LDL: triglyceride-rich lipoproteins, or TRL, and lipoprotein(a), known as Lp(a). Their earlier findings suggested that each of these particles poses a greater risk individually than an LDL particle. As a result, TRL and Lp(a) are considered major contributors to the cardiovascular danger that can persist even after LDL cholesterol has been successfully reduced.

Proteomics is the systematic, large-scale examination and measurement of proteins within cells, tissues, or body fluids at a particular point in time.

While genomics investigates a person's relatively stable DNA, proteomics focuses on proteins, which are constantly responding to changes inside the body. These molecules help construct tissues, process food, transmit hormonal messages, and defend against infections.

Because protein patterns shift in response to factors such as diet, stress, aging, and disease, analyzing them can provide a snapshot of current biological activity. Proteomics can help researchers identify biomarkers, diagnose medical conditions, and track how patients respond to treatment.

Blood Proteins Reveal Hidden Heart Disease Risks

The new investigation focused on proteins, which perform countless essential functions throughout the body. Unlike genetic information, which remains largely unchanged, protein levels and activity can respond to diet, stress, aging, and illness. Studying these changes can therefore provide valuable insights into the body's current biological condition.

Researchers analyzed measurements of nearly 3,000 proteins found in blood samples and compared the results with genetic information and long-term health records. This combination helped them identify protein patterns associated with different lipoprotein particles and provided clues about how those particles might contribute to damage inside the arteries.

One of the most striking discoveries involved TRL and Lp(a). Although these are distinct types of particles, their associated blood protein patterns showed substantial similarities. Those overlapping patterns also appeared to be linked to the later development of heart disease.

The researchers found that these particles carried proteins capable of attracting immune cells, potentially encouraging inflammation and changes in the structure of blood vessel walls. This suggests that TRL and Lp(a) may contribute to cardiovascular disease through some of the same biological processes, rather than simply depositing cholesterol in the arteries.

LDL showed a different pattern. Its associated proteins were primarily connected to cholesterol accumulation in artery walls, highlighting an important distinction between the ways these three types of particles may contribute to heart disease.

"The overlap for TRL and Lp(a) is one of the most interesting findings," says Morze. "It suggests that these particles may affect some of the same inflammatory and vascular processes. This could help identify targets for more precisely directed prevention in the future."

How LDL, TRL, and Lp(a) Affect Heart Health

The three types of lipoproteins examined in the study, LDL, TRL, and Lp(a), transport different amounts of cholesterol and other fats through the bloodstream. Each contains the structural protein apolipoprotein B, or apoB.

All three can contribute to atherosclerosis, a condition in which fatty material accumulates inside blood vessel walls. As these deposits develop, they increase the risk of coronary heart disease.

LDL: Low-density lipoprotein transports most of the cholesterol circulating in the blood. An LDL cholesterol measurement indicates how much cholesterol is contained within these particles.

TRL: Triglyceride-rich lipoproteins transport cholesterol along with triglycerides, which the body uses as an important energy source. As triglycerides are removed from these particles, the remaining fragments can deposit cholesterol inside artery walls. In this study, the TRL measurements primarily reflected liver-produced particles called VLDL and the remnants they leave behind.

Lp(a): Lipoprotein(a) resembles an LDL particle but contains an additional protein known as apolipoprotein(a). Higher levels of Lp(a) are already recognized as an established risk factor for coronary heart disease.

Could the Discovery Lead to Better Heart Disease Prevention?

By identifying biological pathways associated with cardiovascular risk beyond LDL cholesterol, the findings offer researchers new possibilities for developing more personalized prevention strategies. In particular, the shared inflammatory and blood vessel effects linked to TRL and Lp(a) could become promising areas for further investigation.

However, the research did not evaluate a new treatment or establish a blood protein test ready for routine clinical use. Additional studies will be needed to determine whether measuring these biological processes, or developing treatments that target them, can improve protection against heart disease.

"Lowering LDL cholesterol remains a cornerstone of prevention," says Clemens Wittenbecher, who led the study and is Assistant Professor of Precision Medicine and Diagnostics at Chalmers. "Our findings point to biological processes that may help explain some of the risk that remains beyond LDL. The next step is to establish whether measuring or targeting these processes can improve prevention alongside existing treatments."

The results reinforce the importance of controlling LDL cholesterol while revealing why cholesterol reduction alone may not address every source of cardiovascular risk. Understanding the additional ways blood particles affect arteries could ultimately help scientists develop more precise approaches to protecting heart health.

The study, "Apolipoprotein B-containing lipoproteins: distinct roles in atherosclerosis revealed by plasma proteomic profiling," was published in the European Heart Journal.

The research authors are Jakub Morze, Elias Björnson, Michael Y Mi, Martin Adiels, Giorgio E Melloni, Gonçalo Rosas da Silva, Andrzej Rynkiewicz, Nicholas A Marston, Marta Guasch-Ferré, Michael Y Tsai, Jerome I Rotter, Robert E Gerszten, Qi Sun, Frank B Hu, Rikard Landberg, Göran Bergström, Chris J Packard, Jan Borén and Clemens Wittenbecher.

At the time of the research, the authors were affiliated with Chalmers University of Technology and the Institute of Medicine at the University of Gothenburg, Sweden; Harvard Medical School, USA; AstraZeneca, Sweden; Municipal Hospital in Olsztyn, Poland; University of Copenhagen, Denmark; University of Minnesota, USA; The Lundquist Institute at Harbour-UCLA Medical Center in Torrance, USA; Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health in Boston, USA; and the University of Glasgow, UK.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#576. Scientists discover a giant 66-pound rodent that lived 34 million years ago

An ancient rodent of extraordinary size has been identified from fossils uncovered in a mine in southern Thailand. The discovery, made by an international team of paleontologists that includes scientists from McGill University, provides new evidence that giant rodents appeared much earlier and in more parts of the world than previously believed.

The researchers found "remarkably large" fossilized teeth belonging to a previously unknown species called Yakamys sirindhornae. This prehistoric rodent lived approximately 34 million years ago, during the middle of the Paleogene period. Its remains, consisting of a lower second molar and a lower incisor, were recovered from the Bang Mark Pit at the Krabi lignite mine in Thailand's Krabi Province.

Although only two teeth have been identified, they offer valuable clues about rodent gigantism, the evolutionary process through which rodents develop unusually large bodies. The discovery suggests that Southeast Asia played a more significant role in the early evolution of giant rodents than scientists had recognized.

Giant Rodents Have a Much Older History Than Expected

"Today, the largest living rodent is the capybara, but very large rodents are not restricted to recent ecosystems," said Virginie Millien, a biology professor at McGill University and co-author of the study. "Giant rodents evolved repeatedly during the later history of the group, mostly in South America. This discovery suggests that Southeast Asia also played an important role in the early evolutionary history of large-bodied rodents."

The newly identified animal's name reflects both its impressive size and its connection to Thailand. Yakamys combines "yak," the Thai word for giant, with "mys," the Greek word for mouse or rat.

The second part of its scientific name recognizes an important supporter of scientific research in Thailand.

"The species name, sirindhornae, honors Princess Maha Chakri Sirindhorn, in recognition of her longstanding support for science and paleontological research in Thailand," Millien said.

How Big Was This 34-Million-Year-Old Rodent?

To estimate the animal's size, the research team applied seven established methods that use tooth measurements to calculate body mass. Their results suggest that Yakamys sirindhornae weighed approximately 30 kilograms, making it about twice as heavy as other known rodents from the same period, including Hulgana.

That would have made the newly discovered species exceptionally large compared with its contemporaries. However, the researchers emphasize that determining an extinct animal's weight from a small number of fossilized teeth involves considerable uncertainty.

"This is only a ballpark figure, as there is substantial uncertainty associated with fossil body-mass estimation," Millien said.

The limited fossil evidence also makes it difficult to reconstruct how Yakamys evolved or what its life was like. Additional discoveries will be needed to better understand its physical characteristics, ecological role, and evolutionary relationships.

International Research Uncovers Thailand's Prehistoric Past

The fossils were identified through an ongoing international collaboration investigating ancient mammals in Thailand's Krabi Basin. The project brings together scientists from McGill University, Thailand's Department of Mineral Resources, and French research organizations, including CNRS and Université de Poitiers.

By revealing evidence of an unusually large rodent in Southeast Asia 34 million years ago, the discovery expands scientists' understanding of where and when rodent gigantism emerged. Future fossil finds could help establish how these ancient giants lived and how their evolution relates to the enormous rodents that appeared elsewhere in the world.

The research received support from CNRS, Université de Poitiers, Université de Montpellier, McGill University, and the Department of Mineral Resources of Thailand.

Materials provided by McGill University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#573. Pure has zero built-in background checks — how to vet your matches

TL;DR: Pure has no built-in background-check tools and keeps profiles anonymous. Before meeting up in person, verify their photos in the app, look up their number, and run their name through local court dockets yourself.

Mainstream dating apps love to brag about safety. Tinder briefly tried offering background checks through Garbo a few years back, while apps like Bumble and Hinge push selfie verification and real first names to keep profiles honest.

As Jack Dawes outlined in Mashable's guide to anonymous dating, Pure exists to minimize your digital paper trail. You do not need an email, phone number, or full bio on display to swipe. Instead of permanent profiles, users post temporary "dating ads" that vanish after a set window. Conversations self-delete after 24 hours by default, and built-in protections actively block screenshots to keep private photos from leaking.

AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections

Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

Pure — best for anonymous hookups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

That setup makes it a haven for discreet hookups, but it leaves you with a massive safety blind spot: Pure has zero integrated background-checking tools and does not screen users. In fact, the app's legal terms plainly state that it does not run criminal background or identity checks on anyone who signs up.

If you are meeting a stranger from the internet for a casual hookup, you need to know who is walking through the door. Because Pure will not do the vetting for you, here's how to handle the detective work yourself before taking things offline.

Before digging into someone's legal history, you first need to confirm the person chatting with you actually looks like their photos. Catfishing and bot profiles are common across anonymous ad boards, but Pure gives you a few built-in tools to filter out fakes before you share personal contact info:

Look for the Photo Verification badge — Pure lets people take a quick live selfie to earn a verification checkmark, confirming they actually look like their ad photos and aren't using someone else's pictures.

Request a True Photo or hop on a video call — Pure includes a native "True Photo" feature that requests an instant, unfiltered snapshot taken directly through the app's camera. Even better, jump on Pure's built-in video chat. Pure notes in its own safety guidelines that a quick face-to-face video call acts as immediate "face control" — so if a match refuses a two-minute video chat before meeting, take that as an immediate red flag.

Protect yourself during calls — While Pure blocks in-app screenshots and alerts you if someone tries to take one, an external camera can still record a screen. Keep your clothes on during the initial video vibe check until you establish real trust.

If your match has an unverified profile or their photos look like professional studio shots, run them through an image search before getting invested.

Because Pure blocks in-app screenshots inside private chats and alerts the other user if you try to take one, grab a screenshot of their public ad photo from the main feed before opening a conversation, or snap a quick picture of your screen with a second phone or tablet.

Once you have the photo, run it through Google Lens, TinEye, or a facial search tool like FaceCheck.ID. Stolen images from Instagram models, adult content creators, or Reddit threads usually pop up within seconds.

If the photo belongs to an influencer living in another state, do not bother arguing. Tap the three dots inside Pure's chat or ad menu, hit "Report," select "Fake profile," and move on.

You cannot run a meaningful public records search without a real first and last name, an active phone number, or a confirmed social handle. Pure does not require users to link any personal details, so you will eventually have to exchange contact info if you plan to meet in person.

When you do move the conversation off-app, run a few quick digital checks on whatever info they give you:

Check for disposable numbers — Run their 10-digit phone number through a free lookup like NumLookup or Truecaller. This shows whether the number belongs to an actual wireless carrier (like Verizon, AT&T, or T-Mobile) or a disposable virtual VoIP service (like Google Voice, TextNow, or a burner app).

Use Zelle to confirm their legal name — If they give you a real phone number, you can often confirm their identity without spending a dime. Because Zelle connects directly to verified bank accounts, entering the recipient's phone number on Zelle's send screen will usually display the recipient's legal first name and last initial before you approve a payment. Venmo works too, but remember that users can change their Venmo display name to a nickname or fake handle.

Once you have a legal name and a general metro area, you do not need to pay third-party consumer data broker sites for basic safety checks. The most critical public safety records are maintained directly by government agencies and are completely free to search:

County court clerk records — In the U.S., criminal and civil court records are public information managed at the municipal and county levels. Search "[County Name] court records" or "[County Name] clerk of courts" for the area where your match lives. Most county court websites allow you to search public dockets for free, letting you spot active warrants, felony convictions, or domestic violence restraining orders.

The National Sex Offender Public Website (NSOPW.gov) — Run by the U.S. Department of Justice, NSOPW is a free, nationwide registry search tool. You can search by a prospective date's first and last name, city, or zip code to see whether they appear on any state, territorial, or tribal registry.

Be on high alert if a match asks you to prove yourself on an outside website.

A common hookup app scam involves a match claiming that their personal safety app, roommate, or house rules require you to register on an external link before meeting up. The link usually leads to an official-looking site asking for your name, phone number, and a $1 credit card authorization to run a quick check.

The site is a credit card phishing scheme. Pure never requires third-party registration to chat or meet, and legitimate safety services do not ask strangers for payment details to verify an identity. If a match sends you an external verification link, tap the three dots in Pure's chat menu, report the account for "Scam or fraud," and block them.

Pure's safety guidelines also warn against a common scam in which a match asks you to chip in for gas money, an Uber fare, or a taxi to come to you. If someone asks for cash transfers before you meet face-to-face, block them immediately.

When it is time to meet up in person, turn on Pure's built-in Safety Signal tool.

Safety Signal lets you share your date details with a trusted contact before heading out. When you turn it on, Pure generates a password-protected link you can text to a friend. Your friend does not need a Pure account — opening the link in any mobile browser shows your last-known location on a map, your match's profile photos, and an active timer.

Beyond using Safety Signal, keep the basics in mind: meet in a busy, public spot for the first drink, and never rely on your date for a ride. A little planning lets you have fun without taking unnecessary chances.

Discreet hookups are great, but a little healthy snooping ahead of time is the best way to make sure your date stays fun, safe, and completely on your terms.
